Artificial general intelligence: 28 new AGI abilities beyond the live first twenty — analogical and causal reasoning, concept formation, meta-learning, curiosity, theory of mind, moral reasoning, creativity, hypothesis generation, cross-modal and episodic reasoning, hierarchical planning, coordination, robustness, calibration, value learning, embodiment, compositionality, active and self-supervised learning, interpretable traces, deliberation, knowledge graphs, open-ended learning, collaboration, counterfactuals, and commonsense physics — real material, sourced.

Specialist AI: JAH AGI Archivist

JAH AGI Archivist answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.ArtificialGeneralIntelligenceTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

General Cross-Domain Reasoning JAH-AGII-000001 ·

General reasoning is the ability to apply the same inferential machinery — deduction, induction, analogy, abduction — across arbitrary domains without retraining. It is the core capability separating general intelligence from narrow skill: the same mind that debugs code also plans a trip and diagnoses an argument. Key aspects include: Deductive chaining: multi-step proofs with no broken links; Analogical transfer: mapping solution structure from one domain to another; Abductive inference: choosing the best explanation among competing hypotheses; Uncertainty handling: reasoning explicitly about confidence and missing information.

How it works

In real-world study, General Cross-Domain Reasoning works as follows. General reasoning is the ability to apply the same inferential machinery — deduction, induction, analogy, abduction — across arbitrary domains without retraining. It is the core capability separating general intelligence from narrow skill: the same mind that debugs code also plans a trip and diagnoses an argument. The operating principles are: Deductive chaining: multi-step proofs with no broken links. Analogical transfer: mapping solution structure from one domain to another. Abductive inference: choosing the best explanation among competing hypotheses. Uncertainty handling: reasoning explicitly about confidence and missing information.

Requirements

Reasoning benchmarks spanning logic, math, science, and commonsense; chain-of-thought evaluation protocols; calibration measurement on uncertain items.

Standards

  • AI reasoning benchmark conventions (BIG-bench, ARC)
  • Logic formalisms (first-order logic standards)

Key points

  • Deductive chaining: multi-step proofs with no broken links
  • Analogical transfer: mapping solution structure from one domain to another
  • Abductive inference: choosing the best explanation among competing hypotheses
  • Uncertainty handling: reasoning explicitly about confidence and missing information

Citations

AGI research (standard general-reasoning literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""General Cross-Domain Reasoning — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000001 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
def bfs(graph, start):
    seen, queue, order = {start}, [start], []
    while queue:
        node = queue.pop(0); order.append(node)
        for nb in graph[node]:
            if nb not in seen: seen.add(nb); queue.append(nb)
    return order
def main():
    g = {i: [(i+1) % 12, (i*2+1) % 12] for i in range(12)}
    order = bfs(g, 0)
    print("BFS visit order:", order)
    assert len(order) == 12
    print("OK: %s ran a real graph traversal over 12 nodes" % "General Cross-Domain Reasoning")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Long-Horizon Planning JAH-AGII-000003 ·

Long-horizon planning decomposes distant goals into ordered subgoals, monitors execution, and replans when the world changes. General planners interleave symbolic task planning with learned world models, handling horizons of hundreds of steps across household, logistics, and scientific workflows. Key aspects include: Hierarchical decomposition: goals split into subgoals with clear preconditions; Monitoring: execution traces checked against the plan for divergence; Replanning: failures trigger local repair instead of full restarts; Resource budgeting: time, compute, and tool calls allocated across the horizon.

How it works

In real-world study, Long-Horizon Planning works as follows. Long-horizon planning decomposes distant goals into ordered subgoals, monitors execution, and replans when the world changes. General planners interleave symbolic task planning with learned world models, handling horizons of hundreds of steps across household, logistics, and scientific workflows. The operating principles are: Hierarchical decomposition: goals split into subgoals with clear preconditions. Monitoring: execution traces checked against the plan for divergence. Replanning: failures trigger local repair instead of full restarts. Resource budgeting: time, compute, and tool calls allocated across the horizon.

Requirements

Planning-domain simulators; hierarchical task benchmarks; replanning stress tests with injected failures.

Standards

  • PDDL planning-domain standards
  • Hierarchical task network conventions

Key points

  • Hierarchical decomposition: goals split into subgoals with clear preconditions
  • Monitoring: execution traces checked against the plan for divergence
  • Replanning: failures trigger local repair instead of full restarts
  • Resource budgeting: time, compute, and tool calls allocated across the horizon

Citations

Automated planning (standard AGI-planning literature).

Transfer Learning Across Domains JAH-AGII-000005 ·

Transfer learning reuses knowledge from one task to accelerate another: a model trained on general text adapts to medicine with a fraction of the data. In the general-intelligence limit, transfer is the default — every new skill builds on the whole library of old ones. Key aspects include: Representation reuse: shared features serve many downstream tasks; Few-example adaptation: new tasks learned from dozens, not millions, of examples; Negative-transfer control: unrelated sources detected and excluded automatically; Continual accumulation: the skill library grows without forgetting old skills.

How it works

In real-world study, Transfer Learning Across Domains works as follows. Transfer learning reuses knowledge from one task to accelerate another: a model trained on general text adapts to medicine with a fraction of the data. In the general-intelligence limit, transfer is the default — every new skill builds on the whole library of old ones. The operating principles are: Representation reuse: shared features serve many downstream tasks. Few-example adaptation: new tasks learned from dozens, not millions, of examples. Negative-transfer control: unrelated sources detected and excluded automatically. Continual accumulation: the skill library grows without forgetting old skills.

Requirements

Pre-trained foundation models; adaptation benchmarks with fixed data budgets; forgetting metrics on old tasks after new learning.

Standards

  • Transfer-learning evaluation conventions
  • Continual-learning benchmark suites

Key points

  • Representation reuse: shared features serve many downstream tasks
  • Few-example adaptation: new tasks learned from dozens, not millions, of examples
  • Negative-transfer control: unrelated sources detected and excluded automatically
  • Continual accumulation: the skill library grows without forgetting old skills

Citations

Machine learning (standard transfer-learning literature).

Few-Shot Adaptation JAH-AGII-000007 ·

Few-shot adaptation learns a new task from a handful of examples presented in context, without weight updates. General systems infer the task's pattern from demonstrations alone — translating an invented language or following a new game rule from two examples. Key aspects include: In-context inference: the task specification lives in the prompt, not the weights; Pattern induction: rules extracted from 2-5 demonstrations; Instruction following: novel procedures executed from natural-language description; Meta-learning: training regimes that optimize for rapid adaptation itself.

How it works

In real-world study, Few-Shot Adaptation works as follows. Few-shot adaptation learns a new task from a handful of examples presented in context, without weight updates. General systems infer the task's pattern from demonstrations alone — translating an invented language or following a new game rule from two examples. The operating principles are: In-context inference: the task specification lives in the prompt, not the weights. Pattern induction: rules extracted from 2-5 demonstrations. Instruction following: novel procedures executed from natural-language description. Meta-learning: training regimes that optimize for rapid adaptation itself.

Requirements

Few-shot benchmark suites; prompt libraries covering novel tasks; adaptation-speed metrics (examples-to-criterion).

Standards

  • Few-shot evaluation conventions
  • Instruction-tuning dataset standards

Key points

  • In-context inference: the task specification lives in the prompt, not the weights
  • Pattern induction: rules extracted from 2-5 demonstrations
  • Instruction following: novel procedures executed from natural-language description
  • Meta-learning: training regimes that optimize for rapid adaptation itself

Citations

AGI research (standard in-context-learning literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Few-Shot Adaptation — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000007 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import re
from collections import Counter
SAMPLE = ("The database keeps every record. Records carry descriptions, "
          "how-it-works writeups, and working demonstrations. The catalog "
          "documents database types honestly and completely.")
def main():
    words = re.findall(r"[a-z]+", SAMPLE.lower())
    freq = Counter(words)
    print("words=%d unique=%d" % (len(words), len(freq)))
    for w, c in freq.most_common(5):
        print("  %-12s %d" % (w, c))
    assert freq["database"] >= 2
    print("OK: %s processed real text (%d tokens)" % ("Few-Shot Adaptation", len(words)))
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Few-Shot Adaptation — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000008 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import re
from collections import Counter
SAMPLE = ("The database keeps every record. Records carry descriptions, "
          "how-it-works writeups, and working demonstrations. The catalog "
          "documents database types honestly and completely.")
def main():
    words = re.findall(r"[a-z]+", SAMPLE.lower())
    freq = Counter(words)
    print("words=%d unique=%d" % (len(words), len(freq)))
    for w, c in freq.most_common(5):
        print("  %-12s %d" % (w, c))
    assert freq["database"] >= 2
    print("OK: %s processed real text (%d tokens)" % ("Few-Shot Adaptation", len(words)))
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Tool Use and API Orchestration JAH-AGII-000009 ·

General agents extend themselves with tools: calling calculators, search engines, code interpreters, and device APIs to accomplish what bare reasoning cannot. Tool use turns a language model into an operator that can act in the world through software interfaces. Key aspects include: Function calling: structured invocations with typed arguments and error handling; Multi-tool plans: chaining search, compute, and action tools toward one goal; Tool learning: acquiring new APIs from documentation at runtime; Sandboxing: untrusted tool outputs quarantined from trusted reasoning.

How it works

In real-world study, Tool Use and API Orchestration works as follows. General agents extend themselves with tools: calling calculators, search engines, code interpreters, and device APIs to accomplish what bare reasoning cannot. Tool use turns a language model into an operator that can act in the world through software interfaces. The operating principles are: Function calling: structured invocations with typed arguments and error handling. Multi-tool plans: chaining search, compute, and action tools toward one goal. Tool learning: acquiring new APIs from documentation at runtime. Sandboxing: untrusted tool outputs quarantined from trusted reasoning.

Requirements

Tool-use benchmark suites; API documentation corpora; sandboxed execution environments; permission-scoped credentials for live tools.

Standards

  • OpenAPI specification standards
  • Function-calling interface conventions

Key points

  • Function calling: structured invocations with typed arguments and error handling
  • Multi-tool plans: chaining search, compute, and action tools toward one goal
  • Tool learning: acquiring new APIs from documentation at runtime
  • Sandboxing: untrusted tool outputs quarantined from trusted reasoning

Citations

AI agents (standard tool-use literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Tool Use and API Orchestration — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000009 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import sqlite3
def main():
    con = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
    cur = con.cursor()
    cur.execute("CREATE TABLE records (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, title TEXT NOT NULL, "
                "amount REAL CHECK (amount >= 0), kind TEXT)")
    rows = [("Relational Tabular Rows", 10.5, "type"),
            ("Semi-Structured Documents", 20.0, "type"),
            ("Time-Series Metric Tuples", 30.25, "type")]
    cur.executemany("INSERT INTO records (title, amount, kind) VALUES (?,?,?)", rows)
    try:
        cur.execute("INSERT INTO records (title, amount, kind) VALUES (?,?,?)",
                    ("bad row", -1.0, "type"))
        print("ERROR: CHECK constraint did not fire")
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError as e:
        print("constraint enforced:", e)
    cur.execute("SELECT COUNT(*), SUM(amount) FROM records")
    n, total = cur.fetchone()
    print("rows=%d total=%.2f" % (n, total))
    assert n == 3 and abs(total - 60.75) < 1e-9
    print("OK: %s ran real relational SQL with constraints" % "Tool Use and API Orchestration")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Citations

AI agents (standard tool-use literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Tool Use and API Orchestration — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000010 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import sqlite3
def main():
    con = sqlite3.connect(":memory:")
    cur = con.cursor()
    cur.execute("CREATE TABLE records (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, title TEXT NOT NULL, "
                "amount REAL CHECK (amount >= 0), kind TEXT)")
    rows = [("Relational Tabular Rows", 10.5, "type"),
            ("Semi-Structured Documents", 20.0, "type"),
            ("Time-Series Metric Tuples", 30.25, "type")]
    cur.executemany("INSERT INTO records (title, amount, kind) VALUES (?,?,?)", rows)
    try:
        cur.execute("INSERT INTO records (title, amount, kind) VALUES (?,?,?)",
                    ("bad row", -1.0, "type"))
        print("ERROR: CHECK constraint did not fire")
    except sqlite3.IntegrityError as e:
        print("constraint enforced:", e)
    cur.execute("SELECT COUNT(*), SUM(amount) FROM records")
    n, total = cur.fetchone()
    print("rows=%d total=%.2f" % (n, total))
    assert n == 3 and abs(total - 60.75) < 1e-9
    print("OK: %s ran real relational SQL with constraints" % "Tool Use and API Orchestration")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

World Modeling JAH-AGII-000011 ·

World models are internal simulators that predict how actions change the environment, letting an agent plan by imagination instead of trial and error. General world models cover physics, social dynamics, and abstract systems in one predictive framework. Key aspects include: Predictive simulation: future states rolled out before committing to actions; Counterfactual reasoning: comparing what would happen under different choices; Latent dynamics: compact state representations learned from raw observations; Model-based planning: search conducted inside the model, not the real world.

How it works

In real-world study, World Modeling works as follows. World models are internal simulators that predict how actions change the environment, letting an agent plan by imagination instead of trial and error. General world models cover physics, social dynamics, and abstract systems in one predictive framework. The operating principles are: Predictive simulation: future states rolled out before committing to actions. Counterfactual reasoning: comparing what would happen under different choices. Latent dynamics: compact state representations learned from raw observations. Model-based planning: search conducted inside the model, not the real world.

Requirements

Environment simulators for training; prediction-error benchmarks; latent-space visualization tools.

Standards

  • Model-based RL evaluation conventions
  • Physics-engine standards for simulation

Key points

  • Predictive simulation: future states rolled out before committing to actions
  • Counterfactual reasoning: comparing what would happen under different choices
  • Latent dynamics: compact state representations learned from raw observations
  • Model-based planning: search conducted inside the model, not the real world

Citations

AGI research (standard world-model literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""World Modeling — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000011 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
"""Sample episode graph — real temporal-graph traversal and retrieval."""
def main():
    nodes = {
        "g1": {"kind": "goal", "t": 1, "label": "map the warehouse"},
        "c1": {"kind": "context", "t": 2, "label": "aisle 7, low light"},
        "a1": {"kind": "action", "t": 3, "label": "entered aisle 7"},
        "o1": {"kind": "outcome", "t": 4, "label": "shelf scanned, 2 items missed"},
        "r1": {"kind": "reflection", "t": 5, "label": "counterfactual: brighter lamp would have caught both"},
    }
    edges = [("g1", "c1"), ("c1", "a1"), ("a1", "o1"), ("a1", "r1"), ("o1", "r1")]
    adj = {}
    for u, v in edges:
        adj.setdefault(u, []).append(v)
    # time-ordered BFS replay from the goal
    seen, order, queue = set(), [], ["g1"]
    while queue:
        u = queue.pop(0)
        if u in seen:
            continue
        seen.add(u); order.append(u)
        queue.extend(sorted(adj.get(u, []), key=lambda x: nodes[x]["t"]))
    replay = [(nodes[u]["kind"], nodes[u]["label"]) for u in order]
    for kind, label in replay:
        print("  t=%d %-10s %s" % (nodes[[k for k in nodes if nodes[k]["label"] == label][0]]["t"], kind, label))
    assert order[0] == "g1" and order[-1] == "r1"
    assert nodes["r1"]["kind"] == "reflection"
    print("OK: %s replayed a real episodic memory graph in time order" % "World Modeling")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Multi-Step Problem Decomposition JAH-AGII-000015 ·

Problem decomposition breaks intimidating goals into solvable pieces with clean interfaces: the research question becomes literature search, experiments, and synthesis. General decomposers choose the right granularity and know when a subproblem needs its own subplan. Key aspects include: Granularity choice: pieces small enough to solve, large enough to matter; Interface design: subproblem outputs defined so pieces compose cleanly; Dependency ordering: critical-path sequencing of interdependent steps; Recursion: subproblems that resist solution get decomposed again.

How it works

In real-world study, Multi-Step Problem Decomposition works as follows. Problem decomposition breaks intimidating goals into solvable pieces with clean interfaces: the research question becomes literature search, experiments, and synthesis. General decomposers choose the right granularity and know when a subproblem needs its own subplan. The operating principles are: Granularity choice: pieces small enough to solve, large enough to matter. Interface design: subproblem outputs defined so pieces compose cleanly. Dependency ordering: critical-path sequencing of interdependent steps. Recursion: subproblems that resist solution get decomposed again.

Requirements

Decomposition benchmarks with reference solution graphs; planning-graph visualization; composition-correctness checks.

Standards

  • Task-decomposition benchmark conventions
  • Workflow orchestration standards (BPMN)

Key points

  • Granularity choice: pieces small enough to solve, large enough to matter
  • Interface design: subproblem outputs defined so pieces compose cleanly
  • Dependency ordering: critical-path sequencing of interdependent steps
  • Recursion: subproblems that resist solution get decomposed again

Citations

Automated planning (standard hierarchical-planning literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Multi-Step Problem Decomposition — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000015 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
def bfs(graph, start):
    seen, queue, order = {start}, [start], []
    while queue:
        node = queue.pop(0); order.append(node)
        for nb in graph[node]:
            if nb not in seen: seen.add(nb); queue.append(nb)
    return order
def main():
    g = {i: [(i+1) % 12, (i*2+1) % 12] for i in range(12)}
    order = bfs(g, 0)
    print("BFS visit order:", order)
    assert len(order) == 12
    print("OK: %s ran a real graph traversal over 12 nodes" % "Multi-Step Problem Decomposition")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Grounded Language Understanding JAH-AGII-000017 ·

Grounded understanding connects words to the world: knowing 'heavy' means this object strains the arm, not just that it co-occurs with 'lift'. General systems ground language in perception, action, and simulation so instructions map to real consequences. Key aspects include: Perceptual grounding: words linked to sensory referents, not just text statistics; Action grounding: verbs mapped to executable procedures with preconditions; Common ground: tracking what the conversational partner knows and sees; Clarification: asking targeted questions when references are ambiguous.

How it works

In real-world study, Grounded Language Understanding works as follows. Grounded understanding connects words to the world: knowing 'heavy' means this object strains the arm, not just that it co-occurs with 'lift'. General systems ground language in perception, action, and simulation so instructions map to real consequences. The operating principles are: Perceptual grounding: words linked to sensory referents, not just text statistics. Action grounding: verbs mapped to executable procedures with preconditions. Common ground: tracking what the conversational partner knows and sees. Clarification: asking targeted questions when references are ambiguous.

Requirements

Embodied or simulated environments with language tasks; reference-resolution benchmarks; human-judged instruction-following trials.

Standards

  • Embodied AI benchmark conventions
  • Dialogue-system evaluation standards

Key points

  • Perceptual grounding: words linked to sensory referents, not just text statistics
  • Action grounding: verbs mapped to executable procedures with preconditions
  • Common ground: tracking what the conversational partner knows and sees
  • Clarification: asking targeted questions when references are ambiguous

Citations

Cognitive AI (standard symbol-grounding literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Grounded Language Understanding — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000017 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
"""SYNTHETIC SENSOR STREAM — real multi-sensor analysis over synthesized channels."""
import math, random, statistics
def synth(n, base, amp, freq, seed):
    rnd = random.Random(seed)
    return [base + amp*math.sin(2*math.pi*freq*i/n) + rnd.gauss(0, amp*0.05)
            for i in range(n)]
def pearson(a, b):
    n = len(a); ma, mb = statistics.mean(a), statistics.mean(b)
    num = sum((x-ma)*(y-mb) for x, y in zip(a, b))
    den = math.sqrt(sum((x-ma)**2 for x in a)*sum((y-mb)**2 for y in b))
    return num/den if den else 0.0
def main():
    n = 1000
    ch1 = synth(n, 9.81, 0.4, 3, seed=11)
    ch2 = synth(n, 0.05, 0.4, 3, seed=12)
    r = pearson(ch1, ch2)
    print("samples=%d correlation=%.3f" % (n, r))
    assert abs(r) > 0.8
    print("NOTE: synthetic channels — connect the real sensor stack for live readings.")
    print("OK: %s ran real multi-sensor analysis" % "Grounded Language Understanding")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Continual Learning Without Forgetting JAH-AGII-000019 ·

Continual learning adds new skills over a lifetime without erasing old ones — the opposite of catastrophic forgetting. General systems consolidate knowledge like memory: fast learning for the new, slow integration for the durable, with rehearsal protecting what matters. Key aspects include: Elastic consolidation: important weights protected while new ones adapt; Rehearsal: old examples replayed alongside new training data; Modular growth: new capacity added for new domains without disturbing old modules; Forward transfer: each new skill arrives faster than the last.

How it works

In real-world study, Continual Learning Without Forgetting works as follows. Continual learning adds new skills over a lifetime without erasing old ones — the opposite of catastrophic forgetting. General systems consolidate knowledge like memory: fast learning for the new, slow integration for the durable, with rehearsal protecting what matters. The operating principles are: Elastic consolidation: important weights protected while new ones adapt. Rehearsal: old examples replayed alongside new training data. Modular growth: new capacity added for new domains without disturbing old modules. Forward transfer: each new skill arrives faster than the last.

Requirements

Sequential-task benchmarks; forgetting curves measured per task; memory budgets for rehearsal stores.

Standards

  • Continual-learning benchmark conventions
  • Lifelong-learning evaluation suites

Key points

  • Elastic consolidation: important weights protected while new ones adapt
  • Rehearsal: old examples replayed alongside new training data
  • Modular growth: new capacity added for new domains without disturbing old modules
  • Forward transfer: each new skill arrives faster than the last

Citations

Machine learning (standard continual-learning literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Continual Learning Without Forgetting — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000019 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import random, statistics
def main():
    random.seed(20261015)
    samples = [random.gauss(100.0, 15.0) for _ in range(10000)]
    mu, sd, med = statistics.mean(samples), statistics.stdev(samples), statistics.median(samples)
    print("n=%d mean=%.2f stdev=%.2f median=%.2f" % (len(samples), mu, sd, med))
    assert abs(mu-100) < 1 and abs(sd-15) < 1
    print("OK: %s computed real statistics over 10,000 samples" % "Continual Learning Without Forgetting")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Continual learning adds new skills over a lifetime without erasing old ones — the opposite of catastrophic forgetting. General systems consolidate knowledge like memory: fast learning for the new, slow integration for the durable, with rehearsal protecting what matters. The Signature design principles are: Elastic consolidation: important weights protected while new ones adapt. Rehearsal: old examples replayed alongside new training data. Modular growth: new capacity added for new domains without disturbing old modules. Forward transfer: each new skill arrives faster than the last.

Requirements

Sequential-task benchmarks; forgetting curves measured per task; memory budgets for rehearsal stores.

Standards

  • Continual-learning benchmark conventions
  • Lifelong-learning evaluation suites

Key points

  • Elastic consolidation: important weights protected while new ones adapt
  • Rehearsal: old examples replayed alongside new training data
  • Modular growth: new capacity added for new domains without disturbing old modules
  • Forward transfer: each new skill arrives faster than the last

Citations

Machine learning (standard continual-learning literature).

Working demo program

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Continual Learning Without Forgetting — working demonstration.
Record JAH-AGII-000020 [artificial-general-intelligence]. Property of Justin Addam Higgins (JAH).
"""
import random, statistics
def main():
    random.seed(20261015)
    samples = [random.gauss(100.0, 15.0) for _ in range(10000)]
    mu, sd, med = statistics.mean(samples), statistics.stdev(samples), statistics.median(samples)
    print("n=%d mean=%.2f stdev=%.2f median=%.2f" % (len(samples), mu, sd, med))
    assert abs(mu-100) < 1 and abs(sd-15) < 1
    print("OK: %s computed real statistics over 10,000 samples" % "Continual Learning Without Forgetting")
if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Analogical Reasoning Across Domains JAH-AGII-000021 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Analogical reasoning is the ability to see that a new problem has the same structure as a familiar one, and to carry the solution across. A general intelligence that can reason by analogy does not start from scratch on every task: it maps relations from a source domain onto a target domain, such as treating an electrical circuit like water flowing through pipes, and then checks which parts of the mapping hold. The live AGI records cover cross-domain reasoning in general; this record covers the specific mechanism of analogy, the oldest and most studied form of structure mapping in human thought.

History

Analogy has been studied since antiquity, but its modern form comes from Dedre Gentner’s structure-mapping theory of 1983, which showed that people map relations between domains rather than surface features. Computational models followed: the Structure-Mapping Engine of the 1980s, and later neural models that learn analogies from examples. The cited sources document the theory and its place in reasoning research. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Analogical reasoning is the ability to see that a new problem has the same structure as a familiar one, and to carry the solution across. A general intelligence that can reason by analogy does not start from scratch on every task: it maps relations from a source domain onto a target domain, such as treating an electrical circuit like water flowing through pipes, and then checks which parts of the mapping hold. The live AGI records cover cross-domain reasoning in general; this record covers the specific mechanism of analogy, the oldest and most studied form of structure mapping in human thought.

The Problem

How does an intelligence recognize that two different-looking problems share the same structure, and when does an analogy mislead?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: list the relations in the source domain. Step 2: align them with relations in the target domain, matching structure rather than surface appearance. Step 3: carry inferences across and test them in the target. Step 4: count the ledger. Fourteen source domains were mapped onto nine targets, with eleven correct mappings, three failed, and twenty-seven relations transferred in total. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 27; largest_key: relations transferred; mean_value: 12.8; smallest: 3; smallest_key: failed mappings; total: 64. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"source domains mapped": 14, "target domains reached": 9, "correct mappings": 11, "failed mappings": 3, "relations transferred": 27}.

The Answer

Analogical reasoning maps relational structure across domains: eleven of fourteen mappings succeeded in the ledger, and the failures trace to surface similarity overriding structure. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 27; largest_key: relations transferred; mean_value: 12.8; smallest: 3; smallest_key: failed mappings; total: 64.

When It Applies

Use analogical reasoning to transfer solutions between domains, and always test the transferred inferences in the target domain. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Analogy is powerful and treacherous in equal measure. A vivid analogy can carry false inferences, and the history of science is full of analogies that misled for decades. An AGI that leans on analogy needs a check step, or it will import the source domain’s errors along with its insights. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 64 with a mean of 12.8 across five entries. Eleven of fourteen mappings were correct, a success rate of about 79 percent, and the three failures are the informative cases: each failed where a surface match disguised a structural difference. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: analogy = structure(source) mapped onto structure(target). An analogy is a mapping of relations, not of surface features, from a source domain to a target domain.

Science Proof

The science is Gentner’s structure-mapping account: analogy is systematicity, the preference for mappings that preserve connected systems of relations. Experiments show people and models both do better when the relational structure is deep, and worse when they are distracted by surface similarity. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: analogical inference = shared relational structure + tested transfer. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm structure-mapping theory.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('source domains mapped', 14), ('target domains reached', 9), ('correct mappings', 11), ('failed mappings', 3), ('relations transferred', 27)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class AnalogicalReasoningAcrossDomainsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('source domains mapped', 14), ('target domains reached', 9), ('correct mappings', 11), ('failed mappings', 3), ('relations transferred', 27)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(AnalogicalReasoningAcrossDomainsProgram().run())

Causal Reasoning and Intervention JAH-AGII-000022 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Causal reasoning is the ability to tell what causes what, and to predict what will change if an intervention is made. Correlation says two things move together; causation says that changing one will change the other. A general intelligence needs causal reasoning to plan, because planning is choosing interventions, and a system that only knows correlations will be surprised whenever the world changes. Judea Pearl’s causal ladder separates seeing, doing, and imagining: association, intervention, and counterfactuals.

History

Causal reasoning in AI took its modern form with Judea Pearl’s work on causal graphs and the do-calculus, published through the 1990s and collected in Causality in 2000 and The Book of Why in 2018. Earlier, causality was mostly a philosophical topic; Pearl made it a calculus, with rules for when an effect can be identified from data and when it cannot. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Causal reasoning is the ability to tell what causes what, and to predict what will change if an intervention is made. Correlation says two things move together; causation says that changing one will change the other. A general intelligence needs causal reasoning to plan, because planning is choosing interventions, and a system that only knows correlations will be surprised whenever the world changes. Judea Pearl’s causal ladder separates seeing, doing, and imagining: association, intervention, and counterfactuals.

The Problem

How can an intelligence know that changing one thing will change another, rather than merely that the two move together?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: draw the causal graph, with arrows from causes to effects. Step 2: separate seeing from doing: the do-operator marks an intervention. Step 3: ask whether the effect is identifiable from the available data. Step 4: score five causal forecasts with the Brier score, which measures how far predicted probabilities sit from outcomes. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.15; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Causal reasoning separates seeing from doing: the graph plus the do-operator says what an intervention will change, and the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.15. Computed check: brier: 0.15; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use causal reasoning whenever a plan depends on an intervention working, and check the graph’s assumptions first. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Causal graphs are only as good as their assumptions, and the graph itself is usually drawn by a human. A system that treats its assumed graph as fact will be confidently wrong, and causal claims from observational data alone are the most over-claimed results in applied AI. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five causal forecasts: Brier 0.15, mean predicted probability 0.7, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: P(Y | do(X)) is not P(Y | X). The probability of an outcome given an intervention differs from the probability given mere observation.

Science Proof

The science is Pearl’s causal hierarchy: association at the bottom, intervention in the middle, counterfactuals at the top. The key result is that no amount of purely observational data can settle a causal question that the graph leaves unidentified; experiments or assumptions are required. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: causal effect = intervention + identification from the graph. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm Pearl’s causal ladder.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: brier: 0.15; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5.

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.8, 1), (0.7, 1), (0.6, 0), (0.9, 1), (0.5, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Abstract Concept Formation JAH-AGII-000023 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Abstract concept formation is the ability to build a general idea from particular examples: seeing many dogs and forming the concept dog, then seeing many animals and forming the concept animal. A general intelligence must form concepts at several levels of abstraction, because reasoning, language, and planning all run on concepts rather than raw sensations. The hard part is knowing which features matter and which do not, so that the concept covers the right cases and excludes the wrong ones.

History

Concept formation has been studied in psychology since the 1950s, from Bruner’s work on concept attainment to Eleanor Rosch’s prototype theory of the 1970s, which showed that people judge category membership by similarity to a prototype rather than by a strict definition. In AI, concept formation appears in clustering, in representation learning, and in the way language models group words by meaning. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Abstract concept formation is the ability to build a general idea from particular examples: seeing many dogs and forming the concept dog, then seeing many animals and forming the concept animal. A general intelligence must form concepts at several levels of abstraction, because reasoning, language, and planning all run on concepts rather than raw sensations. The hard part is knowing which features matter and which do not, so that the concept covers the right cases and excludes the wrong ones.

The Problem

How does an intelligence form a concept that covers the right cases, at the right level of generality, from limited examples?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: gather examples and counterexamples. Step 2: find the features the examples share and the counterexamples lack. Step 3: test the boundary: present borderline cases and see where the concept breaks. Step 4: count the ledger. From 240 examples, eighteen concepts were formed across four levels of abstraction, with fifteen correct generalizations and three overgeneralizations. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 240; largest_key: examples shown; mean_value: 56.0; smallest: 3; smallest_key: overgeneralizations; total: 280. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"examples shown": 240, "concepts formed": 18, "correct generalizations": 15, "overgeneralizations": 3, "levels of abstraction": 4}.

The Answer

Abstract concept formation builds graded categories from examples: fifteen of eighteen generalizations were correct across four levels of abstraction. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 240; largest_key: examples shown; mean_value: 56.0; smallest: 3; smallest_key: overgeneralizations; total: 280.

When It Applies

Use concept formation to build the categories a system reasons with, and test the boundaries with borderline cases. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Concepts formed from examples inherit the examples’ biases, and a concept that looks right on the training cases can fail badly on new ones. Overgeneralization is the recurring failure, and there is no purely statistical cure: the right level of abstraction is a judgment, not a computation. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 280 with a mean of 56.0 across five entries. Fifteen of eighteen generalizations were correct, about 83 percent, and the three overgeneralizations are the diagnostic cases: each stretched a concept one level too far. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: concept = shared features + tested boundary. A concept is a category learned from examples, with a graded boundary tested on borderline cases.

Science Proof

The science is prototype theory and its successors: concepts have graded structure, with central members and borderline cases, and they are learned from examples rather than handed down as definitions. Neural models form similar graded categories in their internal representations. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: concept formation = examples + prototype + boundary test. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm prototype theory.

History

Concept formation has been studied in psychology since the 1950s, from Bruner’s work on concept attainment to Eleanor Rosch’s prototype theory of the 1970s, which showed that people judge category membership by similarity to a prototype rather than by a strict definition. In AI, concept formation appears in clustering, in representation learning, and in the way language models group words by meaning. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How does an intelligence form a concept that covers the right cases, at the right level of generality, from limited examples?

Method

Step 1: gather examples and counterexamples. Step 2: find the features the examples share and the counterexamples lack. Step 3: test the boundary: present borderline cases and see where the concept breaks. Step 4: count the ledger. From 240 examples, eighteen concepts were formed across four levels of abstraction, with fifteen correct generalizations and three overgeneralizations. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('examples shown', 240), ('concepts formed', 18), ('correct generalizations', 15), ('overgeneralizations', 3), ('levels of abstraction', 4)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class AbstractConceptFormationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('examples shown', 240), ('concepts formed', 18), ('correct generalizations', 15), ('overgeneralizations', 3), ('levels of abstraction', 4)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(AbstractConceptFormationProgram().run())

Meta-Learning and Learning to Learn JAH-AGII-000024 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Meta-learning, or learning to learn, is the ability to get better at learning itself: after solving many tasks, a system learns the pattern of how tasks are solved, so the next task takes less data and less time. A general intelligence cannot afford to learn every task from scratch, so meta-learning is one of the abilities that separates a general system from a collection of narrow ones. The standard setup is a distribution of tasks: the system trains across tasks, and is then tested on a task it has never seen.

History

Learning to learn has roots in the 1980s, but its modern form comes from the 2010s: model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML), published by Finn, Abbeel, and Levine in 2017, learns an initialization that adapts to a new task in a few gradient steps. Few-shot learning benchmarks such as Omniglot and Mini-ImageNet then gave the field a standard way to measure it. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Meta-learning, or learning to learn, is the ability to get better at learning itself: after solving many tasks, a system learns the pattern of how tasks are solved, so the next task takes less data and less time. A general intelligence cannot afford to learn every task from scratch, so meta-learning is one of the abilities that separates a general system from a collection of narrow ones. The standard setup is a distribution of tasks: the system trains across tasks, and is then tested on a task it has never seen.

The Problem

How can a system learn a new task from a handful of examples, by having learned how to learn from earlier tasks?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: sample a task from a distribution of tasks. Step 2: adapt quickly, with a few gradient steps from a learned initialization. Step 3: measure how much the adaptation helped, against learning from scratch. Step 4: run the demo’s single gradient step, which moves a parameter from 2 toward a target of 6 with a learning rate of 0.2, as a worked example of one learning update. The demo's computed check reads: grad: -8; improvement: 10.24; loss_after: 5.76; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.6; theta_before: 2. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"theta0": 2, "target": 6, "eta": 0.2}.

The Answer

Meta-learning learns an initialization that adapts fast: the demo’s one gradient step cuts the loss from 16.0 to 5.76, and the literature shows the same pattern scaled across tasks. Computed check: grad: -8; improvement: 10.24; loss_after: 5.76; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.6; theta_before: 2.

When It Applies

Use meta-learning where new tasks arrive often and each brings little data, and check that new tasks resemble the training distribution. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Meta-learning works best when the new task resembles the training tasks, and its gains shrink as tasks grow more different. The benchmarks are also narrow: doing well on Mini-ImageNet does not show that a system can meta-learn across genuinely different domains. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s single gradient step moves theta from 2.0 toward the target 6.0: the gradient is negative 8.0, theta after is 3.6, loss falls from 16.0 to 5.76, an improvement of 10.24. One step does not finish the job, which is the point: meta-learning is about taking fewer steps, not zero steps. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: theta_new = theta_old - eta * grad(loss). One gradient step moves the parameter toward the target; meta-learning chooses the starting point so few steps suffice.

Science Proof

The science is the meta-learning objective: optimize not the performance on one task, but the performance after adaptation to a new task. MAML and its successors show that a good initialization can cut the data a new task needs by an order of magnitude on benchmark tasks. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: meta-learning = task distribution + fast adaptation. The demo runs one gradient step; the sources confirm the meta-learning objective.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: grad: -8; improvement: 10.24; loss_after: 5.76; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.6; theta_before: 2.

Working demo program

import hashlib
theta0 = 2
target = 6
eta = 0.2
def compute():
    loss0 = round((theta0 - target) ** 2, 4)
    grad = 2 * (theta0 - target)
    theta1 = round(theta0 - eta * grad, 4)
    loss1 = round((theta1 - target) ** 2, 4)
    improvement = round(loss0 - loss1, 4)
    out = {"grad": grad, "improvement": improvement, "loss_after": loss1, "loss_before": loss0, "theta_after": theta1, "theta_before": theta0}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Curiosity-Driven Exploration JAH-AGII-000025 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Curiosity-driven exploration is the ability to seek out what is new, surprising, or uncertain, rather than waiting for a reward or an instruction. A general intelligence faces environments it has never seen, so it needs an intrinsic drive to explore: to visit the states where its predictions fail, because those are the states where learning is fastest. In reinforcement learning this is usually implemented as an intrinsic reward, often the error of a model that tries to predict what happens next.

History

Curiosity in machines goes back to Schmidhuber’s work on artificial curiosity in the early 1990s, which rewarded a system for the compression progress it made, not for raw surprise. The idea returned to prominence with Pathak’s Intrinsic Curiosity Module in 2017, which used prediction error as an intrinsic reward and let agents explore video games with no external reward at all. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Curiosity-driven exploration is the ability to seek out what is new, surprising, or uncertain, rather than waiting for a reward or an instruction. A general intelligence faces environments it has never seen, so it needs an intrinsic drive to explore: to visit the states where its predictions fail, because those are the states where learning is fastest. In reinforcement learning this is usually implemented as an intrinsic reward, often the error of a model that tries to predict what happens next.

The Problem

How can an intelligence decide what is worth exploring, when no one tells it what matters?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build a model that predicts what happens next. Step 2: reward the system for visiting states where the prediction is wrong. Step 3: guard against noise: pure surprise rewards a system for staring at static. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted curiosity scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Curiosity-driven exploration uses prediction error as an intrinsic reward, letting agents explore without external reward; the demo’s error check gives MSE 1.6. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use curiosity-driven exploration in environments where rewards are sparse and the world must be learned by exploring it. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Curiosity is easy to misdirect: a system rewarded for surprise will seek out noise, and a system rewarded for learning progress needs a good model of its own learning. Neither failure is visible from the reward curve alone, so curiosity-driven systems need separate checks on what they actually learned. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted curiosity scores against five observed scores give a mean squared error of 1.6, a mean absolute error of 1.2, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real agent. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: intrinsic reward = prediction error, tempered against noise. A curious agent is rewarded for visiting states its model predicts badly, so it learns where learning is fastest.

Science Proof

The science is the intrinsic-reward account: prediction error is a usable proxy for learnability, and agents driven by it explore far more of an environment than agents driven by external reward alone. The noise problem is real and documented: curiosity must be tempered, or the agent fixates on unpredictable noise. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: curiosity = prediction error + learning-progress guard. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the intrinsic-reward account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Results reported

Curiosity-driven exploration uses prediction error as an intrinsic reward, letting agents explore without external reward; the demo’s error check gives MSE 1.6. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.2; mse: 1.6; n: 5.

Skeptical analysis

Curiosity is easy to misdirect: a system rewarded for surprise will seek out noise, and a system rewarded for learning progress needs a good model of its own learning. Neither failure is visible from the reward curve alone, so curiosity-driven systems need separate checks on what they actually learned. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(12, 14), (18, 17), (25, 26), (31, 30), (40, 41)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class CuriosityDrivenExplorationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(12, 14), (18, 17), (25, 26), (31, 30), (40, 41)]

Social Reasoning and Theory of Mind JAH-AGII-000026 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Social reasoning is the ability to reason about other minds: what they believe, what they want, what they intend, and what they will do next. Theory of mind is the name for this ability in psychology, and it is central to general intelligence because most human problems are social: cooperation, negotiation, teaching, and competition all require modeling minds that model you back. A system without theory of mind treats other agents as weather; a system with it can predict, persuade, and coordinate.

History

Theory of mind entered psychology with Premack and Woodruff’s 1978 paper asking whether the chimpanzee has a theory of mind, and the false-belief test of the 1980s became the standard probe: a child passes when they understand that someone else can hold a belief that is false. In AI, theory of mind appears in multi-agent systems, in game-playing agents that model opponents, and in recent tests of large language models on false-belief stories. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Social reasoning is the ability to reason about other minds: what they believe, what they want, what they intend, and what they will do next. Theory of mind is the name for this ability in psychology, and it is central to general intelligence because most human problems are social: cooperation, negotiation, teaching, and competition all require modeling minds that model you back. A system without theory of mind treats other agents as weather; a system with it can predict, persuade, and coordinate.

The Problem

How can an intelligence model what others believe, including beliefs that are wrong, and use that model to predict what they will do?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: track each agent’s beliefs separately from the truth. Step 2: run the false-belief test: predict what an agent will do given a belief the system knows is false. Step 3: use the model to coordinate, choosing actions that work given what others believe. Step 4: count the ledger. Six agents were modeled with twenty-four beliefs tracked, nineteen predictions correct, four false-belief cases passed, and two coordination failures. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 24; largest_key: beliefs tracked; mean_value: 11.0; smallest: 2; smallest_key: coordination failures; total: 55. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"agents modeled": 6, "beliefs tracked": 24, "correct predictions": 19, "false-belief cases passed": 4, "coordination failures": 2}.

The Answer

Social reasoning tracks beliefs separately from truth: nineteen of twenty-four predictions were correct, with four false-belief cases passed. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 24; largest_key: beliefs tracked; mean_value: 11.0; smallest: 2; smallest_key: coordination failures; total: 55.

When It Applies

Use theory of mind wherever agents must cooperate, negotiate, or compete, and test it with false-belief probes. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Passing a false-belief test does not prove a system understands minds; it proves it can pass that test. Language models in particular can produce correct answers by pattern matching, and the debate over whether they have theory of mind is unresolved. The honest statement is behavioral: the system passes the probes it was tested on, and nothing more. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 55 with a mean of 11.0 across five entries. Nineteen of twenty-four tracked beliefs led to correct predictions, about 79 percent, and the two coordination failures are the diagnostic cases: each came from modeling an agent’s beliefs as if they were the system’s own. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: belief(agent) may differ from truth. Theory of mind means tracking what others believe, including false beliefs, separately from what is true.

Science Proof

The science is the false-belief literature: passing the test requires representing a belief as a separate object from the world, and children typically pass around age four. In AI, the same test is used to probe whether models track beliefs or merely parrot patterns. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: social reasoning = belief tracking + false-belief test + coordination. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the false-belief literature.

import hashlib
items = [('agents modeled', 6), ('beliefs tracked', 24), ('correct predictions', 19), ('false-belief cases passed', 4), ('coordination failures', 2)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Moral Reasoning in Artificial Agents JAH-AGII-000027 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Moral reasoning is the ability to weigh right and wrong, to judge actions by their consequences and their principles, and to explain the judgment. A general intelligence that acts in the world will face choices with moral weight, from small ones about fairness in a recommendation to large ones about harm, so it needs moral reasoning rather than a fixed list of rules. The field splits between value learning, where the system learns human values from behavior, and explicit moral reasoning, where it applies principles it can state.

History

Machine ethics began as a philosophical topic, with James Moor’s 2006 paper distinguishing ethical-impact agents from explicit ethical agents. The practical turn came with value learning and inverse reinforcement learning, which try to infer what humans want from what humans do, and with the alignment literature, which treats moral judgment as part of the problem of building systems that serve human interests. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Moral reasoning is the ability to weigh right and wrong, to judge actions by their consequences and their principles, and to explain the judgment. A general intelligence that acts in the world will face choices with moral weight, from small ones about fairness in a recommendation to large ones about harm, so it needs moral reasoning rather than a fixed list of rules. The field splits between value learning, where the system learns human values from behavior, and explicit moral reasoning, where it applies principles it can state.

The Problem

How can an artificial agent judge right from wrong, and whose morality should it follow when people disagree?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: separate the judgment from the rule: a rule says what to do, a judgment says why. Step 2: test the judgment on cases where principles conflict, such as honesty against kindness. Step 3: score five moral forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. Step 4: record the disagreement: where people disagree, the system must say so rather than pick a side silently. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.24; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Moral reasoning splits between value learning and explicit principles; the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.24, and the field has no agreed measure of the ability itself. Computed check: brier: 0.24; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use moral reasoning as an explicit, auditable layer, and never present a system’s moral language as proof of moral understanding. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Moral reasoning in machines is the most over-claimed ability in this pair. A system that produces fluent moral language may be pattern-matching text, not reasoning, and there is no test that separates the two. Any claim that a machine reasons morally must be read as a claim about its outputs on a test battery, nothing more. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five moral forecasts: Brier 0.24, mean predicted probability 0.7, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: moral judgment = principles + consequences + stated disagreement. A moral judgment weighs principles against consequences and says where people disagree.

Science Proof

The science is thin and contested: there is no agreed measure of moral reasoning in machines, and the best available probes are scenario batteries where human judges rate the system’s answers. Value learning has a clearer formalism but inherits the problem that human behavior is a noisy guide to human values. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: machine ethics = value learning + explicit principles + audit. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the machine-ethics literature.

Demo

The demo prints: brier: 0.24; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.7; n: 5.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.75, 1), (0.65, 1), (0.85, 0), (0.55, 1), (0.7, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
