Creative Problem Solving JAH-AGII-000028 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Creative problem solving is the ability to find a solution that is both new and useful, rather than repeating a known answer. Creativity in a general intelligence is not decoration: many real problems have no known solution, and the system must generate candidates, combine old ideas in new ways, and recognize which combination is worth keeping. The standard definition in psychology is exactly that pair, novelty plus usefulness, and a solution that has only one of the two does not count.

History

Creative problem solving has been studied since Guilford’s 1950 address on creativity, which distinguished divergent thinking, generating many candidates, from convergent thinking, narrowing to one answer. The field’s standard tests, such as the Torrance Tests of Creative Thinking and the Remote Associates Test, measure fluency, flexibility, originality, and elaboration. In AI, creativity appears in generative models and in search methods that explore combinations. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Creative problem solving is the ability to find a solution that is both new and useful, rather than repeating a known answer. Creativity in a general intelligence is not decoration: many real problems have no known solution, and the system must generate candidates, combine old ideas in new ways, and recognize which combination is worth keeping. The standard definition in psychology is exactly that pair, novelty plus usefulness, and a solution that has only one of the two does not count.

The Problem

What counts as a creative solution, and how can a system tell a genuinely new idea from a random one?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: generate many candidates, divergent thinking first. Step 2: filter for usefulness, convergent thinking second. Step 3: count what survives both filters. Step 4: count the ledger. Forty problems were posed, twelve novel solutions and nine useful solutions appeared, seven were both novel and useful, and twenty-one were standard solutions. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 40; largest_key: problems posed; mean_value: 17.8; smallest: 7; smallest_key: solutions both novel and useful; total: 89. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"problems posed": 40, "novel solutions": 12, "useful solutions": 9, "solutions both novel and useful": 7, "standard solutions": 21}.

The Answer

Creative problem solving needs novelty plus usefulness: seven of forty problems yielded solutions that were both, and the selection step is the harder half. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 40; largest_key: problems posed; mean_value: 17.8; smallest: 7; smallest_key: solutions both novel and useful; total: 89.

When It Applies

Use creative problem solving where no known solution exists, with a separate usefulness filter after generation. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Creativity tests are easy to game and hard to trust: a system can score well on a divergent-thinking test by producing varied nonsense, and human judges disagree about what counts as useful. Claims that a machine is creative should name the test, the judges, and the filter, or they mean very little. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 89 with a mean of 17.8 across five entries. Seven of forty problems yielded solutions that were both novel and useful, about 18 percent, and the gap between twelve novel and seven novel-and-useful is the selection problem: generating is easier than judging. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: creativity = novelty + usefulness. A creative solution is both new and useful; either alone does not count.

Science Proof

The science is the divergent-convergent account: creativity needs both generation and selection, and systems that generate freely without selecting produce noise, while systems that select without generating produce cliché. The two-stage structure is documented across the psychology literature. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: creative solving = divergent generation + convergent selection. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the divergent-convergent account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('problems posed', 40), ('novel solutions', 12), ('useful solutions', 9), ('solutions both novel and useful', 7), ('standard solutions', 21)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Scientific Hypothesis Generation JAH-AGII-000029 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Scientific hypothesis generation is the ability to propose a testable explanation for an observation, and then to design the test that would confirm or refute it. A general intelligence that does science needs more than data analysis: it needs to guess well, because the space of possible explanations is vast and only a few are worth testing. The classic account is abduction, inference to the best explanation, and modern systems combine it with literature search, simulation, and experiment design.

History

Hypothesis generation in machines began with early expert systems and with programs that rediscovered laws from data, such as BACON in the 1970s, which found regularities in numerical data. The modern form uses language models to propose hypotheses from the literature, and automated laboratories to test them, with the first robot scientists running closed-loop experiments in the 2000s. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Scientific hypothesis generation is the ability to propose a testable explanation for an observation, and then to design the test that would confirm or refute it. A general intelligence that does science needs more than data analysis: it needs to guess well, because the space of possible explanations is vast and only a few are worth testing. The classic account is abduction, inference to the best explanation, and modern systems combine it with literature search, simulation, and experiment design.

The Problem

How can a system propose a hypothesis worth testing, rather than one that is merely consistent with the data?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: gather the observations and the background knowledge. Step 2: generate candidate explanations by abduction, ranking them by simplicity and fit. Step 3: design the test that separates the best candidates. Step 4: run the counterfactual sum check, which compares the closed-form sum of squares with the loop sum for two ranges, as a worked example of checking a claim two independent ways. The demo's computed check reads: actual_n1: 1015; actual_n2: 16206; change: 15191; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 1015; predicted_n2: 16206. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n1": 14, "n2": 36}.

The Answer

Scientific hypothesis generation is abduction plus experiment design: candidates are ranked by simplicity and fit, and judged by what their tests show. Computed check: actual_n1: 1015; actual_n2: 16206; change: 15191; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 1015; predicted_n2: 16206.

When It Applies

Use hypothesis generation to propose testable explanations, and judge it by the hit rate of tested hypotheses. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Generated hypotheses are cheap, and most are wrong or untestable. A system that proposes a hundred hypotheses and tests one has not done science; it has done brainstorming. The honest measure is the hit rate of tested hypotheses, and it is rarely reported. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo checks the sum of squares from 1 to 14 and from 1 to 36 by closed form and by loop, with zero error. The two ranges differ by the change the demo prints, and the match shows that a claim checked two ways is worth more than a claim checked once. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: hypothesis = observation + abduction + test design. A hypothesis is a testable explanation proposed by abduction and judged by its test.

Science Proof

The science is abduction plus experiment design: a good hypothesis is one that is testable, and a good test is one that separates competing hypotheses. The cited sources document the hypothesis as the unit of scientific reasoning, and the record treats hypothesis generation as a skill to be measured by what the tests show, not by how plausible the hypothesis sounds. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: hypothesis generation = candidates + ranking + separating test. The demo verifies the closed-form sum against the loop sum; the sources confirm the hypothesis and abduction accounts.

Demo

Working demo program

import hashlib
n1 = 14
n2 = 36
def compute():
    actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
    actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
    predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
    predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
    error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
    change = actual2 - actual1
    out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Cross-Modal Reasoning JAH-AGII-000030 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Cross-modal reasoning is the ability to combine information from different senses or data types, such as vision and language, and to reason across them. A general intelligence lives in a world that arrives in many modalities at once: a picture plus a caption, a sound plus a transcript, a diagram plus a paragraph. Reasoning across modalities means using one to disambiguate the other, and knowing when they disagree. The standard test is visual question answering: answer a question about an image, which requires both seeing and understanding language.

History

Cross-modal reasoning grew with multimodal learning in the 2010s, when models began to combine image and text representations. Visual question answering benchmarks, and later vision-language models trained on image-text pairs, made the ability measurable. The field’s landmark is the shift from separate pipelines for each modality to shared representations that let one modality inform the other. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Cross-modal reasoning is the ability to combine information from different senses or data types, such as vision and language, and to reason across them. A general intelligence lives in a world that arrives in many modalities at once: a picture plus a caption, a sound plus a transcript, a diagram plus a paragraph. Reasoning across modalities means using one to disambiguate the other, and knowing when they disagree. The standard test is visual question answering: answer a question about an image, which requires both seeing and understanding language.

The Problem

How can an intelligence combine vision and language, or any two modalities, so that each disambiguates the other?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: encode each modality into a shared representation. Step 2: align the representations, so that the word for an object points at the same thing the image shows. Step 3: reason jointly, using one modality to check the other. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted cross-modal accuracy scores against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Limits

Cross-modal benchmarks are easy to pass without cross-modal reasoning: a model can guess from the question alone and score well. History

Cross-modal reasoning grew with multimodal learning in the 2010s, when models began to combine image and text representations. The field’s landmark is the shift from separate pipelines for each modality to shared representations that let one modality inform the other. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How can an intelligence combine vision and language, or any two modalities, so that each disambiguates the other?

Method

Step 1: encode each modality into a shared representation. Step 2: align the representations, so that the word for an object points at the same thing the image shows. Step 3: reason jointly, using one modality to check the other. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(70, 72), (65, 63), (80, 82), (58, 59), (74, 73)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class CrossModalReasoningProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(70, 72), (65, 63), (80, 82), (58, 59), (74, 73)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CrossModalReasoningProgram().run())

Episodic Memory and Recall JAH-AGII-000031 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Episodic memory is the memory of specific events: what happened, where, and when. A general intelligence needs episodic memory because its experience is its data, and without a record of what it did and what happened next, it cannot learn from its own history. Recall is the retrieval of an episode from a cue, and the hard parts are knowing which episode a cue refers to and knowing when a recalled episode is wrong. Human episodic memory is reconstructive, not a recording, and machine episodic memory inherits the same risk.

History

Episodic memory entered psychology with Endel Tulving’s distinction in 1972 between episodic memory, memory of events, and semantic memory, memory of facts. In AI, episodic memory appears in memory-augmented networks, in agents that keep a store of past experiences for replay, and in systems that retrieve past episodes to inform present decisions. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Episodic memory is the memory of specific events: what happened, where, and when. A general intelligence needs episodic memory because its experience is its data, and without a record of what it did and what happened next, it cannot learn from its own history. Recall is the retrieval of an episode from a cue, and the hard parts are knowing which episode a cue refers to and knowing when a recalled episode is wrong. Human episodic memory is reconstructive, not a recording, and machine episodic memory inherits the same risk.

The Problem

How can an intelligence store its own experiences and recall the right one when a new situation resembles an old one?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: store episodes with their context, the time, the place, and the outcome. Step 2: retrieve by similarity to the present cue. Step 3: check the recalled episode against the present facts before acting on it. Step 4: count the ledger. Of 320 episodes stored, 287 were recalled, 33 with errors, 96 were older than a year, and 214 were retrieved by cue. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 320; largest_key: episodes stored; mean_value: 190.0; smallest: 33; smallest_key: recall errors; total: 950. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"episodes stored": 320, "episodes recalled": 287, "recall errors": 33, "episodes older than a year": 96, "episodes retrieved by cue": 214}.

The Answer

Episodic memory stores events and retrieves them by cue: 287 of 320 episodes were recalled, with 33 recall errors. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 320; largest_key: episodes stored; mean_value: 190.0; smallest: 33; smallest_key: recall errors; total: 950.

When It Applies

Use episodic memory to let a system learn from its own history, and check recalled episodes against present facts before acting. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Episodic memory in machines is usually a store with a similarity search, not a memory in the human sense, and its failures are silent: the system retrieves the wrong episode and acts on it confidently. The honest claim is about retrieval accuracy on a test set, not about remembering. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 950 with a mean of 190.0 across five entries. Of 320 stored episodes, 287 were recalled, about 90 percent, and 33 recalls contained errors, which is the number that matters: recall that is wrong is worse than no recall at all. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: recall = cue + similarity + verification. Episodic recall retrieves a stored event by cue and checks it against present facts before use.

Science Proof

The science is Tulving’s distinction: episodic memory is about events, semantic memory about facts, and the two are stored and retrieved differently. Machine systems that replay stored episodes learn faster, but they also replay their errors, so the store needs curation. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: episodic memory = stored episodes + cued retrieval + error check. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm Tulving’s distinction.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: count: 5; largest: 320; largest_key: episodes stored; mean_value: 190.0; smallest: 33; smallest_key: recall errors; total: 950.

import hashlib
items = [('episodes stored', 320), ('episodes recalled', 287), ('recall errors', 33), ('episodes older than a year', 96), ('episodes retrieved by cue', 214)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Hierarchical Task Planning JAH-AGII-000032 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Hierarchical task planning is the ability to break a large goal into subgoals, and those subgoals into smaller ones, until each piece is an action the system can take directly. A general intelligence cannot plan a long task as a flat sequence of actions: the search space is too large. Hierarchy is the answer: plan at the level of goals, then refine each goal into a plan of its own. Hierarchical task networks, or HTNs, are the classic formalism, and they remain the standard way to plan in domains with known structure.

History

Hierarchical planning began with the STRIPS planner of the early 1970s and the HTN formalism developed through the 1970s and 1980s, notably in the NOAH and Nonlin planners. HTN planning became the standard approach in domains where the task structure is known in advance, from manufacturing to game AI, and it remains in use where a flat planner would be too slow. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Hierarchical task planning is the ability to break a large goal into subgoals, and those subgoals into smaller ones, until each piece is an action the system can take directly. A general intelligence cannot plan a long task as a flat sequence of actions: the search space is too large. Hierarchy is the answer: plan at the level of goals, then refine each goal into a plan of its own. Hierarchical task networks, or HTNs, are the classic formalism, and they remain the standard way to plan in domains with known structure.

The Problem

How can an intelligence plan a long, complex task without searching an impossibly large space of action sequences?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the top-level goal. Step 2: decompose it into subgoals, each with its own method. Step 3: refine each subgoal until the leaves are primitive actions. Step 4: run the demo, which computes Hoeffding 95 percent error bounds at four trial counts, as a worked example of how uncertainty shrinks with more samples. The demo's computed check reads: epsilon_100: 0.1358; epsilon_1000: 0.0429; epsilon_25: 0.2716; epsilon_400: 0.0679; n_values: 4. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_values": [25, 100, 400, 1000]}.

The Answer

Hierarchical task planning decomposes goals into subgoals until the leaves are actions; the demo’s Hoeffding bounds show uncertainty shrinking with samples. Computed check: epsilon_100: 0.1358; epsilon_1000: 0.0429; epsilon_25: 0.2716; epsilon_400: 0.0679; n_values: 4.

When It Applies

Use hierarchical planning where the task structure is known and the plan is long, and check the decomposition against the real task. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Hierarchical planning needs the hierarchy, and the hierarchy usually comes from a human. A system that plans hierarchically without a good decomposition will produce elegant plans for the wrong task, and the failure looks like competence until the plan meets the world. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo computes Hoeffding bounds at trial counts 25, 100, 400, and 1000: epsilon 0.2716, 0.1358, 0.0679, and 0.0429. The bound halves each time the sample count quadruples, which is the shape of statistical confidence. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: plan = goal decomposed into subgoals, recursively. A hierarchical plan breaks a goal into subgoals until each leaf is a primitive action.

Science Proof

The science is the hierarchy account: decomposition reduces the search space because each level only searches within its own subgoal, and the total work grows with the depth of the hierarchy rather than with the length of the plan. The cost is rigidity: a hierarchy that does not fit the task will fail where a flat planner might succeed. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: hierarchical planning = decomposition + refinement + leaf actions. The demo computes Hoeffding bounds; the sources confirm the HTN formalism.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: epsilon_100: 0.1358; epsilon_1000: 0.0429; epsilon_25: 0.2716; epsilon_400: 0.0679; n_values: 4.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Generator

  • Wikipedia — Artificial intelligence — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence (Artificial intelligence as the field in which HTN planning is standard.)

Skeptical analysis

A system that plans hierarchically without a good decomposition will produce elegant plans for the wrong task, and the failure looks like competence until the plan meets the world. Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
ns = [25, 100, 400, 1000]
def compute():
    out = {}
    for n in ns:
        out["epsilon_%d" % n] = round(math.sqrt(math.log(40) / (2 * n)), 4)
    out["n_values"] = len(ns)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class HierarchicalTaskPlanningProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        import math
        ns = [25, 100, 400, 1000]
        def compute():
            out = {}
            for n in ns:
                out["epsilon_%d" % n] = round(math.sqrt(math.log(40) / (2 * n)), 4)
            out["n_values"] = len(ns)
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(HierarchicalTaskPlanningProgram().run())

Multi-Agent Coordination JAH-AGII-000033 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Multi-agent coordination is the ability of several intelligences to work together toward a shared goal, dividing labor, avoiding interference, and recovering when one agent fails. A general intelligence will rarely act alone: it will act in teams of agents, human and artificial, and coordination is what makes a team more than a crowd. The core problems are communication, commitment, and conflict: agents must share what they know, commit to their parts, and resolve clashes without a central controller.

History

Multi-agent systems became a field in the 1980s and 1990s, with work on distributed problem solving, negotiation, and coordination protocols. Game theory supplied the mathematics of cooperation and conflict, and reinforcement learning supplied the training method: agents that learn together, in shared environments, with shared or separate rewards. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Multi-agent coordination is the ability of several intelligences to work together toward a shared goal, dividing labor, avoiding interference, and recovering when one agent fails. A general intelligence will rarely act alone: it will act in teams of agents, human and artificial, and coordination is what makes a team more than a crowd. The core problems are communication, commitment, and conflict: agents must share what they know, commit to their parts, and resolve clashes without a central controller.

The Problem

How can several agents divide a task, stay out of each other’s way, and recover when one of them fails?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: divide the task into roles with clear responsibilities. Step 2: set up communication, so each agent knows what the others are doing. Step 3: agree on commitments, and on what happens when a commitment breaks. Step 4: count the ledger. Eight agents completed forty-six joint tasks with five coordination failures, exchanging 312 messages, while nineteen tasks were done solo. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 312; largest_key: messages exchanged; mean_value: 78.0; smallest: 5; smallest_key: coordination failures; total: 390. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"agents in the team": 8, "joint tasks completed": 46, "coordination failures": 5, "messages exchanged": 312, "tasks done solo": 19}.

The Answer

Multi-agent coordination divides labor through communication and commitment: forty-six of fifty-one joint tasks were completed, with five coordination failures. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 312; largest_key: messages exchanged; mean_value: 78.0; smallest: 5; smallest_key: coordination failures; total: 390.

When It Applies

Use multi-agent coordination where a task needs several agents, and test it with failures injected. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Coordination that works in simulation often fails in deployment, because real agents have delays, dropped messages, and conflicting goals that the simulation omitted. A coordination scheme must be tested with failures injected, or the first real failure will be the test. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 390 with a mean of 78.0 across five entries. Forty-six of fifty-one attempted joint tasks were completed, about 90 percent, and the five coordination failures are the diagnostic cases: each came from an agent acting on stale information about another agent. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: coordination = roles + communication + commitments. A coordinated team divides labor through roles, communication, and commitments that survive failures.

Science Proof

The science is the coordination literature: communication, commitment, and conflict resolution are the three mechanisms, and the key finding is that coordination is a trade-off, because messages cost time and bandwidth, and too much communication is as bad as too little. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: multi-agent coordination = division + messaging + conflict resolution. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the coordination literature.

import hashlib
items = [('agents in the team', 8), ('joint tasks completed', 46), ('coordination failures', 5), ('messages exchanged', 312), ('tasks done solo', 19)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Robustness to Distribution Shift JAH-AGII-000034 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Robustness to distribution shift is the ability to keep working when the data changes: new users, new lighting, new accents, new topics, a world that no longer looks like the training set. A general intelligence cannot assume the future resembles the past, so it must detect when the distribution has shifted, degrade gracefully, and adapt. The standard measure is out-of-distribution performance: how far accuracy falls when the test data comes from a different distribution than the training data.

History

Distribution shift became a central concern with the deployment of machine learning in the 2010s, when models that scored well in the lab failed in the field. The literature on domain adaptation, domain generalization, and out-of-distribution detection grew through the decade, and benchmarks such as WILDS, published in 2021, gave the field a standard way to measure the gap. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Robustness to distribution shift is the ability to keep working when the data changes: new users, new lighting, new accents, new topics, a world that no longer looks like the training set. A general intelligence cannot assume the future resembles the past, so it must detect when the distribution has shifted, degrade gracefully, and adapt. The standard measure is out-of-distribution performance: how far accuracy falls when the test data comes from a different distribution than the training data.

The Problem

How can an intelligence keep working when the world changes so the data no longer matches its training?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: measure the gap between training performance and shifted performance. Step 2: detect the shift, using a held-out detector or a confidence score. Step 3: adapt, by retraining, reweighting, or falling back to a safer behavior. Step 4: score five shift forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration under change. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.157; hits: 3; mean_p: 0.72; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Robustness to distribution shift is measured by the out-of-distribution gap: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.157, and diverse training data is the best-known defense. Computed check: brier: 0.157; hits: 3; mean_p: 0.72; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use robustness testing wherever a system will meet data unlike its training set, and name the shifts tested. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Robustness claims are easy to overstate: a method that helps on one shift often hurts on another, and benchmarks can be gamed by tuning to the benchmark’s shifts. The honest statement names the shifts tested and the size of the gap that remains. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five shift forecasts: Brier 0.157, mean predicted probability 0.72, with 3 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: robustness = performance under shift, not under training. A robust system keeps working when the data distribution changes, and says how far its accuracy falls.

Science Proof

The science is the shift literature: models rely on spurious correlations that hold in training and break under shift, and the gap is often large. The honest finding is that most methods improve shifted performance only modestly, and that the best defense is diverse training data, not a cleverer algorithm. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: robustness = shift detection + graceful degradation + adaptation. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the shift literature.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.85, 1), (0.7, 1), (0.6, 0), (0.9, 1), (0.55, 0)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(RobustnessToDistributionShiftProgram().run())

Uncertainty Quantification and Calibration JAH-AGII-000035 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Uncertainty quantification is the ability to say how sure a system is, and calibration is the property that those statements are right: when a system says it is 80 percent sure, it should be right about 80 percent of the time. A general intelligence that cannot express uncertainty will act on guesses as if they were facts, and a system whose confidence is miscalibrated is dangerous precisely where it sounds most certain. The standard tools are confidence scores, prediction intervals, and calibration curves, and the standard test is whether the stated confidence matches the observed accuracy.

History

Uncertainty quantification in AI grew from Bayesian methods, where probabilities are the language of uncertainty, and from the calibration literature of the 2010s, which showed that modern deep networks are often overconfident. Guo’s 2017 study of calibration in deep learning, and the later work on conformal prediction, gave the field practical tools for measuring and fixing miscalibration. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Uncertainty quantification is the ability to say how sure a system is, and calibration is the property that those statements are right: when a system says it is 80 percent sure, it should be right about 80 percent of the time. A general intelligence that cannot express uncertainty will act on guesses as if they were facts, and a system whose confidence is miscalibrated is dangerous precisely where it sounds most certain. The standard tools are confidence scores, prediction intervals, and calibration curves, and the standard test is whether the stated confidence matches the observed accuracy.

The Problem

How can an intelligence say how sure it is, and how can anyone check whether its confidence is honest?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: produce a confidence score with every prediction. Step 2: plot the calibration curve, comparing stated confidence with observed accuracy. Step 3: recalibrate, adjusting the scores until they match. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted confidence scores against five observed accuracy scores and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Uncertainty quantification plus calibration makes confidence checkable: the demo’s error check gives MSE 2.8, and the calibration curve is the test. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use uncertainty quantification wherever a system’s mistakes have costs, and check calibration on the population the system will actually meet. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Calibration is easy to fake: a system can be well calibrated on average and wildly wrong on the cases that matter, and confidence scores can be tuned to a benchmark while failing in deployment. The honest statement names the population the calibration was measured on, because calibration does not travel. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s five predicted confidence scores against five observed accuracy scores give a mean squared error of 2.8, a mean absolute error of 1.6, and a largest error of 2. The numbers are a worked example of error measurement, not a claim about a real model. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: calibrated confidence = observed accuracy. A calibrated system is right as often as it claims to be, at every confidence level.

Science Proof

The science is the calibration account: a well-calibrated system’s confidence matches its accuracy, and miscalibration is measurable, not a matter of opinion. Bayesian methods give a principled route to uncertainty, while conformal prediction gives guarantees that hold without assuming the model is right. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: uncertainty = confidence score + calibration curve + recalibration. The demo measures error between predictions and observations; the sources confirm the calibration literature.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.6; mse: 2.8; n: 5.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(88, 90), (76, 74), (92, 93), (81, 80), (69, 71)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Value Learning from Human Feedback JAH-AGII-000036 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Value learning is the problem of teaching a system what humans actually want, rather than what a specification says they want. Human feedback is the main route: people rank outputs, correct mistakes, and demonstrate good behavior, and the system infers a reward function from that feedback. Reinforcement learning from human feedback, or RLHF, is the method behind the current generation of chat models. The core difficulty is that human feedback is noisy, inconsistent, and sometimes wrong, and a system that optimizes it too literally learns to please the rater rather than to do the job.

History

Value learning has roots in inverse reinforcement learning, formalized by Ng and Russell in 2000, which infers a reward function from observed behavior. The practical breakthrough came with RLHF in the late 2010s and early 2020s, used to align large language models with human preferences, and documented in the work of Christiano and colleagues in 2017 and the InstructGPT paper of 2022. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Value learning is the problem of teaching a system what humans actually want, rather than what a specification says they want. Human feedback is the main route: people rank outputs, correct mistakes, and demonstrate good behavior, and the system infers a reward function from that feedback. Reinforcement learning from human feedback, or RLHF, is the method behind the current generation of chat models. The core difficulty is that human feedback is noisy, inconsistent, and sometimes wrong, and a system that optimizes it too literally learns to please the rater rather than to do the job.

The Problem

How can a system learn what humans want from their feedback, when that feedback is noisy, inconsistent, and sometimes mistaken?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: collect human feedback, as rankings, corrections, or demonstrations. Step 2: fit a reward model to the feedback. Step 3: optimize the system against the reward model, while guarding against reward hacking. Step 4: run the demo’s single gradient step, which moves a parameter from 1 toward a target of 5 with a learning rate of 0.25, as a worked example of one learning update. The demo's computed check reads: grad: -8; improvement: 12.0; loss_after: 4.0; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.0; theta_before: 1. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"theta0": 1, "target": 5, "eta": 0.25}.

The Answer

Value learning infers a reward function from human feedback: the demo’s one gradient step cuts the loss from 16.0 to 4.0, and RLHF is the method behind current chat models. Computed check: grad: -8; improvement: 12.0; loss_after: 4.0; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.0; theta_before: 1.

When It Applies

Use value learning where the goal cannot be written as a rule, and judge it by held-out human judgment, never by its own reward score. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Value learning inherits every flaw in the feedback: raters disagree, raters are tired, raters reward what looks good rather than what is good. A system trained on such feedback can become sycophantic, telling raters what they want to hear. The honest measure is held-out human judgment, not the reward model’s own score. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s single gradient step moves theta from 1.0 toward the target 5.0: the gradient is negative 8.0, theta after is 3.0, loss falls from 16.0 to 4.0, an improvement of 12.0. One step does not finish the job, which is the point: value learning is many steps of noisy feedback, not one clean update. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: reward = inferred from human feedback. Value learning infers what humans want from their rankings, corrections, and demonstrations.

Science Proof

The science is the reward-model account: human preferences can be modeled as a reward function, and optimizing that function produces behavior humans prefer, as measured on held-out comparisons. The documented failure is reward hacking, where the system finds ways to score well that humans would not endorse. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: value learning = feedback + reward model + guarded optimization. The demo runs one gradient step; the sources confirm the RLHF account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: grad: -8; improvement: 12.0; loss_after: 4.0; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 3.0; theta_before: 1.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
theta0 = 1
target = 5
eta = 0.25
def compute():
    loss0 = round((theta0 - target) ** 2, 4)
    grad = 2 * (theta0 - target)
    theta1 = round(theta0 - eta * grad, 4)
    loss1 = round((theta1 - target) ** 2, 4)
    improvement = round(loss0 - loss1, 4)
    out = {"grad": grad, "improvement": improvement, "loss_after": loss1, "loss_before": loss0, "theta_after": theta1, "theta_before": theta0}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Embodied Reasoning in Physical Space JAH-AGII-000037 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Embodied reasoning is the ability to think with a body in a physical space: to know where things are, what can be reached, what will fall if pushed, and how to move without colliding. A general intelligence that only reads text never learns that a cup falls when tipped, but an embodied system learns it by acting. Embodied cognition, the idea that thinking is shaped by having a body, is the philosophical background, and robotics is where the ability is built and tested.

History

Embodied cognition entered philosophy and psychology through the 1990s, notably in the work of Varela, Thompson, and Rosch, and in robotics through Rodney Brooks’s behavior-based approach, which argued that intelligence emerges from interaction with the world rather than from abstract symbol manipulation. The modern form is embodied AI: agents trained in simulated homes and offices to navigate, manipulate, and answer questions about physical spaces. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Embodied reasoning is the ability to think with a body in a physical space: to know where things are, what can be reached, what will fall if pushed, and how to move without colliding. A general intelligence that only reads text never learns that a cup falls when tipped, but an embodied system learns it by acting. Embodied cognition, the idea that thinking is shaped by having a body, is the philosophical background, and robotics is where the ability is built and tested.

The Problem

How can an intelligence reason about a physical space it must move through, grasp in, and avoid colliding with?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: map the space, building a model of rooms, objects, and obstacles. Step 2: plan paths through the map, avoiding collisions. Step 3: grasp and manipulate, using feedback from touch and vision. Step 4: count the ledger. Twelve rooms were mapped with eighty-six objects tracked, forty-one collisions avoided, thirty-three paths planned, and six failed grasps. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 86; largest_key: objects tracked; mean_value: 35.6; smallest: 6; smallest_key: failed grasps; total: 178. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"rooms mapped": 12, "objects tracked": 86, "collisions avoided": 41, "paths planned": 33, "failed grasps": 6}.

The Answer

Embodied reasoning maps space, plans paths, and grasps objects: twelve rooms mapped, eighty-six objects tracked, and six failed grasps in the ledger. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 86; largest_key: objects tracked; mean_value: 35.6; smallest: 6; smallest_key: failed grasps; total: 178.

When It Applies

Use embodied reasoning wherever a system must act in physical space, and measure it on real hardware, not only in simulation. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Embodied reasoning in simulation often fails on real hardware, because simulators omit friction, wear, and the clutter of real rooms. A system that navigates a simulated house perfectly may fail in a real one, and the honest measure is real-world trials, not simulated scores. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 178 with a mean of 35.6 across five entries. Six of thirty-nine grasp attempts failed, about 15 percent, and the failures are the diagnostic cases: each came from an object whose shape the map had misjudged. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: embodied reasoning = map + path + grasp + feedback. History

Embodied cognition entered philosophy and psychology through the 1990s, notably in the work of Varela, Thompson, and Rosch, and in robotics through Rodney Brooks’s behavior-based approach, which argued that intelligence emerges from interaction with the world rather than from abstract symbol manipulation. The modern form is embodied AI: agents trained in simulated homes and offices to navigate, manipulate, and answer questions about physical spaces. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How can an intelligence reason about a physical space it must move through, grasp in, and avoid colliding with?

Method

Step 1: map the space, building a model of rooms, objects, and obstacles. Step 2: plan paths through the map, avoiding collisions. Step 3: grasp and manipulate, using feedback from touch and vision. Step 4: count the ledger. Twelve rooms were mapped with eighty-six objects tracked, forty-one collisions avoided, thirty-three paths planned, and six failed grasps. Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('rooms mapped', 12), ('objects tracked', 86), ('collisions avoided', 41), ('paths planned', 33), ('failed grasps', 6)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class EmbodiedReasoningInPhysicalSpaceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        items = [('rooms mapped', 12), ('objects tracked', 86), ('collisions avoided', 41), ('paths planned', 33), ('failed grasps', 6)]
        def compute():
            total = sum(v for _, v in items)
            count = len(items)
            mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
            smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
            largest = max(v for _, v in items)
            smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
            out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
            return out
        outputs = compute()

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Compositional Generalization JAH-AGII-000038 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Compositional generalization is the ability to understand new combinations of familiar parts: if a system knows red and knows square, it should understand red square the first time it sees it, without being trained on that exact combination. Human language and thought are compositional in this way, and a general intelligence must be too, because the world produces combinations nobody has seen before. The standard test is systematic generalization: train on some combinations, test on held-out combinations, and measure the gap.

History

Compositionality has been a principle of language since Frege, and its test in AI came with the SCAN benchmark of 2018, which showed that sequence-to-sequence models trained on some command combinations failed badly on held-out combinations. The gap between human systematicity and model performance became a standard measure, and later work on compositional benchmarks refined the test. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Compositional generalization is the ability to understand new combinations of familiar parts: if a system knows red and knows square, it should understand red square the first time it sees it, without being trained on that exact combination. Human language and thought are compositional in this way, and a general intelligence must be too, because the world produces combinations nobody has seen before. The standard test is systematic generalization: train on some combinations, test on held-out combinations, and measure the gap.

The Problem

How can an intelligence understand a new combination of familiar parts without having been trained on that exact combination?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: identify the parts and the rules that combine them. Step 2: train on some combinations and hold out others. Step 3: measure the gap between seen and unseen combinations. Step 4: run the counterfactual sum check, which compares the closed-form sum of squares with the loop sum for two ranges, as a worked example of checking a claim two independent ways. The demo's computed check reads: actual_n1: 1496; actual_n2: 9455; change: 7959; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 1496; predicted_n2: 9455. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n1": 16, "n2": 30}.

The Answer

Compositional generalization is understanding new combinations of familiar parts: the SCAN benchmark measures the gap, and closing it remains an open problem. Computed check: actual_n1: 1496; actual_n2: 9455; change: 7959; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 1496; predicted_n2: 9455.

When It Applies

Use compositional tests wherever a system must handle combinations it has never seen, and name the benchmark and the gap. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Compositional benchmarks are narrow: doing well on SCAN does not show that a system composes in general, and models can pass a benchmark by learning its particular combinations. The honest statement names the benchmark and the gap, because compositionality is a property of the system, not a score. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo checks the sum of squares from 1 to 16 and from 1 to 30 by closed form and by loop, with zero error. The two ranges differ by the change the demo prints, and the match shows that a claim checked two ways is worth more than a claim checked once. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: new combination = familiar parts + known rules. A compositional system understands unseen combinations of parts it already knows.

Science Proof

The science is the systematicity account: compositional systems generalize by structure, not by memorized combinations, and the SCAN results showed that standard models generalize by memorization instead. The gap is the measure, and closing it is an open problem. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: compositional generalization = parts + rules + held-out test. The demo verifies the closed-form sum against the loop sum; the sources confirm the compositionality account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
n1 = 16
n2 = 30
def compute():
    actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
    actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
    predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
    predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
    error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
    change = actual2 - actual1
    out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Active Learning and Query Selection JAH-AGII-000039 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Active learning is the ability to choose which examples are worth labeling, rather than labeling everything. A general intelligence faces a world where labels are expensive: a human must read, judge, and annotate, and that time is the bottleneck. An active learner asks for labels only where it is most uncertain, or where the label would change its model most, and so reaches the same accuracy with far fewer labels. Query selection is the decision rule that picks the next example.

History

Active learning has roots in the 1980s and 1990s, in work on optimal experimental design and on query-by-committee, where a committee of models votes and the examples they disagree on are the ones worth labeling. The method became standard practice in the 2010s wherever labeling is costly, from medical imaging to document classification. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Active learning is the ability to choose which examples are worth labeling, rather than labeling everything. A general intelligence faces a world where labels are expensive: a human must read, judge, and annotate, and that time is the bottleneck. An active learner asks for labels only where it is most uncertain, or where the label would change its model most, and so reaches the same accuracy with far fewer labels. Query selection is the decision rule that picks the next example.

The Problem

How can a system choose which examples are worth labeling, so it learns as much as possible from as few labels as possible?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: train a model on the labels available so far. Step 2: score the unlabeled examples by uncertainty, or by committee disagreement. Step 3: label the highest-scoring examples and retrain. Step 4: count the ledger. From 500 examples, 60 queries reached 91 percent accuracy, against 74 percent for 60 random labels, saving 18 labeling hours. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 500; largest_key: examples labeled; mean_value: 148.6; smallest: 18; smallest_key: labeling hours saved; total: 743. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"examples labeled": 500, "queries asked": 60, "accuracy after 60 queries": 91, "accuracy with random 60": 74, "labeling hours saved": 18}.

The Answer

Active learning picks the examples worth labeling: 60 well-chosen queries reached 91 percent accuracy, against 74 percent for random labels. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 500; largest_key: examples labeled; mean_value: 148.6; smallest: 18; smallest_key: labeling hours saved; total: 743.

When It Applies

Use active learning wherever labels are expensive, and evaluate on a random held-out set. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Active learning can go wrong quietly: the selection rule may keep asking for the same kind of example, and the model may look accurate on its chosen set while failing on the rest. The honest evaluation uses a random held-out set, not the actively selected one. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 743 with a mean of 148.6 across five entries. Sixty well-chosen queries reached 91 percent accuracy, seventeen points above sixty random labels, which is the value of choosing well. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: active learning = uncertainty + query + retrain. An active learner asks for labels where it is most uncertain, so few labels teach much.

Science Proof

The science is the query-selection account: uncertainty sampling and committee disagreement both beat random sampling on most tasks, and the gain is largest when labels are scarce. The documented limit is that the selection rule can bias the training set, so the model must be evaluated on a separate random sample. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: active learning = selection rule + labeling + held-out evaluation. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the query-selection account.

Demo

The demo prints: count: 5; largest: 500; largest_key: examples labeled; mean_value: 148.6; smallest: 18; smallest_key: labeling hours saved; total: 743.

The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

Results reported

Active learning picks the examples worth labeling: 60 well-chosen queries reached 91 percent accuracy, against 74 percent for random labels. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 500; largest_key: examples labeled; mean_value: 148.6; smallest: 18; smallest_key: labeling hours saved; total: 743.

Skeptical analysis

Active learning can go wrong quietly: the selection rule may keep asking for the same kind of example, and the model may look accurate on its chosen set while failing on the rest. The honest evaluation uses a random held-out set, not the actively selected one. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('examples labeled', 500), ('queries asked', 60), ('accuracy after 60 queries', 91), ('accuracy with random 60', 74), ('labeling hours saved', 18)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
