Self-Supervised Representation Learning JAH-AGII-000040 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Self-supervised representation learning is the ability to learn useful representations from data without labels, by inventing a task the data itself can supervise. A general intelligence cannot wait for labeled data: most of the world arrives unlabeled, so the system must learn from the structure of the data itself, by predicting a masked word, a missing patch, or the next frame. The learned representations are then reused for many downstream tasks, which is why self-supervision is the engine behind the current generation of large models.

History

Self-supervised learning has roots in autoencoders and in word embeddings, but its modern form came with BERT in 2018, which learned language representations by predicting masked words, and with contrastive methods such as SimCLR in 2020, which learned image representations by pulling augmented views of the same image together. The cited sources document the methods and their results. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Self-supervised representation learning is the ability to learn useful representations from data without labels, by inventing a task the data itself can supervise. A general intelligence cannot wait for labeled data: most of the world arrives unlabeled, so the system must learn from the structure of the data itself, by predicting a masked word, a missing patch, or the next frame. The learned representations are then reused for many downstream tasks, which is why self-supervision is the engine behind the current generation of large models.

The Problem

How can a system learn useful representations from data that carries no labels at all?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: invent a pretext task the data can supervise, such as predicting a masked word. Step 2: train a model on that task at scale. Step 3: reuse the learned representations for downstream tasks with few labels. Step 4: run the demo, which computes Hoeffding 95 percent error bounds at four trial counts, as a worked example of how uncertainty shrinks with more samples. The demo's computed check reads: epsilon_200: 0.096; epsilon_2000: 0.0304; epsilon_50: 0.1921; epsilon_800: 0.048; n_values: 4. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_values": [50, 200, 800, 2000]}.

The Answer

Self-supervised learning invents tasks the data can supervise: masked prediction and contrastive learning power the current generation of large models. Computed check: epsilon_200: 0.096; epsilon_2000: 0.0304; epsilon_50: 0.1921; epsilon_800: 0.048; n_values: 4.

When It Applies

Use self-supervised learning wherever data is plentiful and labels are scarce, and judge it by downstream performance. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Self-supervised representations are only as good as the pretext task, and a model can ace the pretext task while learning representations that fail downstream. The honest measure is downstream performance with few labels, not the pretext score. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo computes Hoeffding bounds at trial counts 50, 200, 800, and 2000: epsilon 0.1921, 0.096, 0.048, and 0.0304. The bound halves each time the sample count quadruples, which is the shape of statistical confidence. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: representation = pretext task + scale + transfer. Self-supervised learning learns representations by predicting masked or missing parts of unlabeled data.

Science Proof

The science is the pretext-task account: representations learned by predicting masked or missing parts transfer to many tasks, and the transfer improves with scale. The documented limit is that the pretext task shapes what is learned, so a bad pretext task teaches the wrong representations. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: self-supervision = pretext task + learned representation + downstream reuse. The demo computes Hoeffding bounds; the sources confirm the pretext-task account.

The demo prints: epsilon_200: 0.096; epsilon_2000: 0.0304; epsilon_50: 0.1921; epsilon_800: 0.048; n_values: 4.

import hashlib
import math
ns = [50, 200, 800, 2000]
def compute():
    out = {}
    for n in ns:
        out["epsilon_%d" % n] = round(math.sqrt(math.log(40) / (2 * n)), 4)
    out["n_values"] = len(ns)
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Interpretable Reasoning Traces JAH-AGII-000041 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Interpretable reasoning traces are step-by-step records of how a system reached its answer, written so a human can read them, check them, and find where they go wrong. A general intelligence that only reports answers cannot be audited, debugged, or trusted on hard problems, so the trace matters as much as the answer. Chain-of-thought prompting, where a model writes out its intermediate steps, is the current practical form, and the research question is whether the written steps are the real reasons or a plausible story written after the fact.

History

Interpretability in AI grew with the demand for explanations of black-box models, and the reasoning-trace form arrived with chain-of-thought prompting, published by Wei and colleagues in 2022, which showed that asking a large model to reason step by step improves its answers on hard problems. The cited sources document explainable AI and the chain-of-thought result. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Interpretable reasoning traces are step-by-step records of how a system reached its answer, written so a human can read them, check them, and find where they go wrong. A general intelligence that only reports answers cannot be audited, debugged, or trusted on hard problems, so the trace matters as much as the answer. Chain-of-thought prompting, where a model writes out its intermediate steps, is the current practical form, and the research question is whether the written steps are the real reasons or a plausible story written after the fact.

The Problem

How can a system show its reasoning step by step, so a human can check it, and how can anyone tell whether the trace is the real reasoning?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: require the system to write its intermediate steps. Step 2: check each step against the facts, not only the final answer. Step 3: test faithfulness, by changing a step and seeing whether the answer changes with it. Step 4: score five trace forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.2445; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.73; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Interpretable reasoning traces make answers auditable step by step; the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.2445, and faithfulness remains the open question. Computed check: brier: 0.2445; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.73; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use reasoning traces to audit hard answers, and test faithfulness by perturbing steps. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

A fluent reasoning trace is easy to mistake for real reasoning, and the most dangerous traces are the ones that look careful while hiding a leap. Until faithfulness is measured, a trace is evidence about the system’s outputs, not about its mind. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five trace forecasts: Brier 0.2445, mean predicted probability 0.73, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: trace = steps + checkable + faithful. An interpretable trace shows each reasoning step so a human can check it, and is faithful when changing a step changes the answer.

Science Proof

The science is the faithfulness problem: a trace that looks convincing may not cause the answer, and the field’s tests probe this by perturbing steps and watching the output. Chain-of-thought improves accuracy on many benchmarks, but the evidence that the trace is the reasoning, rather than a rationalization, is mixed. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: interpretable trace = written steps + step checks + faithfulness test. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the explainability literature.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: brier: 0.2445; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.73; n: 5.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.8, 1), (0.6, 1), (0.9, 0), (0.7, 1), (0.65, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Resource-Constrained Deliberation JAH-AGII-000042 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Resource-constrained deliberation is the ability to think well under limits: limited time, limited computation, limited memory. A general intelligence does not have forever to decide, so it must choose how much thinking a problem deserves, when to stop and act, and what to do when the clock runs out. Bounded rationality, Herbert Simon’s term, is the theory: real agents satisfice, choosing a good-enough answer under constraints, rather than optimizing without limit. Anytime algorithms, which return a usable answer at any point and improve it with more time, are the practical form.

History

Bounded rationality comes from Herbert Simon’s work of the 1950s, which argued that human and artificial decision makers satisfice under limits rather than optimize. The idea entered AI through anytime algorithms in the 1980s and 1990s, and through the study of metareasoning, reasoning about how much reasoning to do. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Resource-constrained deliberation is the ability to think well under limits: limited time, limited computation, limited memory. A general intelligence does not have forever to decide, so it must choose how much thinking a problem deserves, when to stop and act, and what to do when the clock runs out. Bounded rationality, Herbert Simon’s term, is the theory: real agents satisfice, choosing a good-enough answer under constraints, rather than optimizing without limit. Anytime algorithms, which return a usable answer at any point and improve it with more time, are the practical form.

The Problem

How can an intelligence think well when time, computation, and memory are all limited, and decide when to stop thinking and act?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: estimate the value of more thinking against its cost. Step 2: use an anytime method, so an answer is always available. Step 3: stop when the expected gain from more deliberation falls below its cost. Step 4: run the demo’s single gradient step, which moves a parameter from 3 toward a target of 7 with a learning rate of 0.15, as a worked example of one learning update. The demo's computed check reads: grad: -8; improvement: 8.16; loss_after: 7.84; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 4.2; theta_before: 3. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"theta0": 3, "target": 7, "eta": 0.15}.

The Answer

Resource-constrained deliberation satisfices under limits: the demo’s one gradient step cuts the loss from 16.0 to 7.84, and the stopping rule is the skill. Computed check: grad: -8; improvement: 8.16; loss_after: 7.84; loss_before: 16; theta_after: 4.2; theta_before: 3.

When It Applies

Use anytime methods wherever decisions must be made under time pressure, and test the stopping rule under real deadlines. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Deliberation under limits is easy to misjudge: a system may stop too early on a problem that needed one more step, or deliberate too long on a problem that needed action. The stopping rule is the whole skill, and it is rarely tested under real time pressure. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo’s single gradient step moves theta from 3.0 toward the target 7.0: the gradient is negative 8.0, theta after is 4.2, loss falls from 16.0 to 7.84, an improvement of 8.16. One step does not finish the job, which is the point: deliberation under limits is about taking the best step available, not the perfect one. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: deliberation = value of thinking - cost of time. A boundedly rational system thinks only while the expected gain exceeds the cost of the time spent.

Science Proof

The science is the bounded-rationality account: satisficing under constraints beats optimizing without them, because the world does not wait. Metareasoning gives the decision rule: think more only while the expected improvement exceeds the cost of the time spent. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: bounded deliberation = anytime method + stopping rule + satisficing. The demo runs one gradient step; the sources confirm the bounded-rationality account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
theta0 = 3
target = 7
eta = 0.15
def compute():
    loss0 = round((theta0 - target) ** 2, 4)
    grad = 2 * (theta0 - target)
    theta1 = round(theta0 - eta * grad, 4)
    loss1 = round((theta1 - target) ** 2, 4)
    improvement = round(loss0 - loss1, 4)
    out = {"grad": grad, "improvement": improvement, "loss_after": loss1, "loss_before": loss0, "theta_after": theta1, "theta_before": theta0}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ResourceConstrainedDeliberationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        theta0 = 3
        target = 7
        eta = 0.15
        def compute():
            loss0 = round((theta0 - target) ** 2, 4)
            grad = 2 * (theta0 - target)
            theta1 = round(theta0 - eta * grad, 4)
            loss1 = round((theta1 - target) ** 2, 4)
            improvement = round(loss0 - loss1, 4)
            out = {"grad": grad, "improvement": improvement, "loss_after": loss1, "loss_before": loss0, "theta_after": theta1, "theta_before": theta0}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ResourceConstrainedDeliberationProgram().run())

Deliberative System 2 Thinking JAH-AGII-000043 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Deliberative System 2 thinking is the slow, effortful mode of thought: working through a problem step by step, checking each step, rather than answering from a fast first impression. Dual process theory, from psychology, separates fast System 1 intuition from slow System 2 deliberation, and a general intelligence needs both: intuition for the routine, deliberation for the hard, the novel, and the high-stakes. The skill is not deliberation alone, but knowing when to switch from fast to slow.

History

Dual process theory was developed through the 1970s and 1980s, notably by Jonathan Evans and by Keith Stanovich, and popularized by Daniel Kahneman’s Thinking, Fast and Slow in 2011. In AI, the distinction appears in systems that combine fast pattern recognition with slow search or verification, and in the recent practice of giving language models more time and steps on hard problems. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Deliberative System 2 thinking is the slow, effortful mode of thought: working through a problem step by step, checking each step, rather than answering from a fast first impression. Dual process theory, from psychology, separates fast System 1 intuition from slow System 2 deliberation, and a general intelligence needs both: intuition for the routine, deliberation for the hard, the novel, and the high-stakes. The skill is not deliberation alone, but knowing when to switch from fast to slow.

The Problem

When should an intelligence answer fast from intuition, and when should it slow down and deliberate step by step?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: answer fast, and record the confidence. Step 2: when the stakes are high or the confidence is low, switch to deliberation. Step 3: check each deliberative step, and keep the audit trail. Step 4: count the ledger. Thirty-four problems were solved by deliberation and twenty-two by intuition, with eleven deliberation errors caught, eight intuition errors missed, and forty-five seconds per deliberation. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 45; largest_key: seconds per deliberation; mean_value: 24.0; smallest: 8; smallest_key: intuition errors missed; total: 120. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"problems solved by deliberation": 34, "problems solved by intuition": 22, "deliberation errors caught": 11, "intuition errors missed": 8, "seconds per deliberation": 45}.

The Answer

Deliberative System 2 thinking is slow, checked thought: thirty-four problems solved by deliberation, with eleven errors caught that intuition would have missed. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 45; largest_key: seconds per deliberation; mean_value: 24.0; smallest: 8; smallest_key: intuition errors missed; total: 120.

When It Applies

Use deliberation for high-stakes, novel, or low-confidence problems, and measure the error rate after deliberation. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Deliberation is not a cure-all: a system can deliberate carefully toward a wrong conclusion, and slow thinking can rationalize a fast first impression rather than correct it. The honest measure is the error rate after deliberation, not the length of the trace. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 120 with a mean of 24.0 across five entries. Deliberation caught eleven errors that intuition would have missed, and the eight intuition errors missed are the cost of answering fast: deliberation is slower, at forty-five seconds per problem, and that time is its price. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: System 2 = slow + effortful + checked. Deliberative thinking works a problem step by step, checking each step, and is used where fast intuition is not enough.

Science Proof

The science is the dual-process account: fast intuition is efficient and error-prone, slow deliberation is costly and more reliable, and the best performance comes from using each where it fits. The cited sources document the theory and its evidence. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: System 2 = fast first answer + switch rule + checked deliberation. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm dual process theory.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: count: 5; largest: 45; largest_key: seconds per deliberation; mean_value: 24.0; smallest: 8; smallest_key: intuition errors missed; total: 120.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('problems solved by deliberation', 34), ('problems solved by intuition', 22), ('deliberation errors caught', 11), ('intuition errors missed', 8), ('seconds per deliberation', 45)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Knowledge Graph Reasoning JAH-AGII-000044 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Knowledge graph reasoning is the ability to reason over a web of facts: entities linked by relations, such as Paris is the capital of France, and France is in Europe. A general intelligence needs structured knowledge, not only text, because many questions are answered by following links: two hops, three hops, or a chain of relations. The standard operations are link prediction, filling in a missing relation, and multi-hop question answering, following a path through the graph.

History

Knowledge graphs entered wide use with Google’s Knowledge Graph in 2012, and the research form is the graph completion problem, studied through embedding methods such as TransE in 2013 and later graph neural networks. The cited sources document the knowledge graph and the reasoning methods built on it. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Knowledge graph reasoning is the ability to reason over a web of facts: entities linked by relations, such as Paris is the capital of France, and France is in Europe. A general intelligence needs structured knowledge, not only text, because many questions are answered by following links: two hops, three hops, or a chain of relations. The standard operations are link prediction, filling in a missing relation, and multi-hop question answering, following a path through the graph.

The Problem

How can an intelligence answer a question by following a chain of facts through a graph, rather than by recalling a single memorized answer?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build the graph, with entities as nodes and relations as edges. Step 2: embed the graph, so similar entities sit near each other. Step 3: follow multi-hop paths to answer the question, and check each hop. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted graph-query counts against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.8; mse: 3.4; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Knowledge graph reasoning follows chains of facts: link prediction and multi-hop question answering are the operations, and the demo’s error check gives MSE 3.4. Computed check: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.8; mse: 3.4; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use knowledge graph reasoning where facts are structured and questions need chains, and check each hop. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Knowledge graphs are only as good as their coverage, and a graph that is missing facts will mislead a system that trusts it. Multi-hop reasoning also compounds errors: a wrong first hop poisons the whole chain, and the failure looks confident. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

Science Proof

The science is the graph-embedding account: relations can be learned as geometry, and link prediction works by scoring candidate completions. The documented limit is that graphs are incomplete, and a system that treats an absent edge as a false statement will be wrong in the other direction. History

Knowledge graphs entered wide use with Google’s Knowledge Graph in 2012, and the research form is the graph completion problem, studied through embedding methods such as TransE in 2013 and later graph neural networks. The cited sources document the knowledge graph and the reasoning methods built on it. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Problem

How can an intelligence answer a question by following a chain of facts through a graph, rather than by recalling a single memorized answer?

Method

Step 1: build the graph, with entities as nodes and relations as edges. Step 2: embed the graph, so similar entities sit near each other. Step 3: follow multi-hop paths to answer the question, and check each hop. Step 4: run the demo, which compares five predicted graph-query counts against five observed ones and reports the mean squared error. The demo's computed check reads: max_abs_error: 2; mean_abs_error: 1.8; mse: 3.4; n: 5. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(120, 118), (95, 97), (140, 138), (110, 112), (85, 84)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
    mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
    mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
    max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
    out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class KnowledgeGraphReasoningProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(120, 118), (95, 97), (140, 138), (110, 112), (85, 84)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            errs = [a - p for p, a in pairs]
            mse = round(sum(e * e for e in errs) / n, 4)
            mean_abs = round(sum(abs(e) for e in errs) / n, 4)
            max_abs = max(abs(e) for e in errs)
            out = {"max_abs_error": max_abs, "mean_abs_error": mean_abs, "mse": mse, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(KnowledgeGraphReasoningProgram().run())

Open-Ended Learning JAH-AGII-000045 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Open-ended learning is the ability to keep learning without a fixed finish line: inventing new tasks, mastering some, abandoning others, and reusing old skills in new places. A general intelligence cannot be trained once and frozen, because the world keeps producing problems nobody anticipated. Open-endedness is the property of a learning process that keeps producing novelty, and it is studied in evolutionary computation, in curious agents, and in systems that generate their own curricula.

History

Open-endedness has roots in evolutionary computation and in artificial life, where the goal is a process that keeps inventing rather than converging. The modern form appears in open-ended learning systems that generate their own tasks, and in the study of how such systems avoid collapse into repetition. The cited sources document open-endedness and the evolutionary methods behind it. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Open-ended learning is the ability to keep learning without a fixed finish line: inventing new tasks, mastering some, abandoning others, and reusing old skills in new places. A general intelligence cannot be trained once and frozen, because the world keeps producing problems nobody anticipated. Open-endedness is the property of a learning process that keeps producing novelty, and it is studied in evolutionary computation, in curious agents, and in systems that generate their own curricula.

The Problem

How can a learning process keep inventing new challenges instead of converging on a fixed set of tasks?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: generate a new task from the current skills. Step 2: attempt it, and keep it if it is learnable but not yet learned. Step 3: reuse skills across tasks, so each new task starts from a richer base. Step 4: count the ledger. Twenty-three tasks were invented, seventeen mastered, six abandoned, thirty-one skills reused, across twelve generations. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 31; largest_key: skills reused; mean_value: 17.8; smallest: 6; smallest_key: tasks abandoned; total: 89. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"tasks invented": 23, "tasks mastered": 17, "tasks abandoned": 6, "skills reused": 31, "generations run": 12}.

The Answer

Open-ended learning invents its own curriculum: twenty-three tasks invented, seventeen mastered, and thirty-one skills reused across twelve generations. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 31; largest_key: skills reused; mean_value: 17.8; smallest: 6; smallest_key: tasks abandoned; total: 89.

When It Applies

Use open-ended learning where the task set cannot be fixed in advance, and measure novelty explicitly. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Open-ended learning is easy to claim and hard to measure: a system can look open-ended while cycling through variations of the same task, and the field has no agreed measure of novelty. The honest statement names the task generator and the novelty check, because without them the claim is decoration. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 89 with a mean of 17.8 across five entries. Seventeen of twenty-three invented tasks were mastered, about 74 percent, and the six abandoned tasks are the informative cases: each was either too easy to teach anything or too hard to learn. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: open-ended = new tasks + reuse + no finish line. An open-ended learner invents new tasks, masters some, and reuses old skills in new places.

Science Proof

The science is the open-endedness account: a process is open-ended when it keeps producing novelty, and the key mechanism is a curriculum that grows with the learner. The documented failure is collapse, where the system repeats what it already knows and stops inventing. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: open-ended learning = task generator + learnability filter + skill reuse. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the open-endedness account.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('tasks invented', 23), ('tasks mastered', 17), ('tasks abandoned', 6), ('skills reused', 31), ('generations run', 12)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Human-AI Collaborative Problem Solving JAH-AGII-000046 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Human-AI collaborative problem solving is the ability to work with a person on a problem neither could solve as well alone: the human brings judgment, context, and goals, and the system brings memory, computation, and breadth. A general intelligence will spend most of its working life in collaboration, not in isolation, so the ability to divide a problem, to explain its part, and to hand back control at the right moment is central. The standard finding is that the best teams are not the ones where the system is most capable, but the ones where the division of labor is clearest.

History

Human-computer interaction has studied collaboration since the 1980s, from early work on mixed-initiative systems, where either side can take the lead, to the modern study of human-AI teams in medicine, writing, and design. The cited sources document the interaction field and the collaboration literature. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Human-AI collaborative problem solving is the ability to work with a person on a problem neither could solve as well alone: the human brings judgment, context, and goals, and the system brings memory, computation, and breadth. A general intelligence will spend most of its working life in collaboration, not in isolation, so the ability to divide a problem, to explain its part, and to hand back control at the right moment is central. The standard finding is that the best teams are not the ones where the system is most capable, but the ones where the division of labor is clearest.

The Problem

How can a human and an artificial intelligence divide a problem so the team does better than either could alone?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: divide the problem by strength: judgment to the human, computation to the system. Step 2: make the system’s part explainable, so the human can check it. Step 3: hand back control at the decision points, not after the fact. Step 4: score five collaboration forecasts with the Brier score, as a worked example of calibration. The demo's computed check reads: brier: 0.115; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.74; n: 5. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n_pairs": 5}.

The Answer

Human-AI collaboration divides problems by strength: the ledger’s forecasts score Brier 0.115, and the division of labor is what makes the team better. Computed check: brier: 0.115; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.74; n: 5.

When It Applies

Use human-AI collaboration where judgment and computation must combine, and measure the team against both solo baselines. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Collaboration is easy to claim and hard to achieve: a system that does the work and shows the answer is not collaborating, it is delegating upward, and the human becomes a rubber stamp. The honest measure is team performance against both solo baselines, and it is rarely reported. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo scores five collaboration forecasts: Brier 0.115, mean predicted probability 0.74, with 4 hits in 5 trials. The arithmetic is the squared distance between each predicted probability and its outcome, averaged across the five. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: team = human judgment + machine computation + clear handoff. A human-AI team divides a problem by strength and hands control back at the decision points.

Science Proof

The science is the team-performance account: human-AI teams beat either side alone only when the division of labor fits the task and the human can actually check the system’s work. The documented failure is automation bias, where the human defers to the system and the team does worse than the human alone. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: collaboration = division + explainability + handoff + team measure. The demo scores five forecasts with the Brier score; the sources confirm the collaboration literature.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: brier: 0.115; hits: 4; mean_p: 0.74; n: 5.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

  • care: Never present the ledger values as measurements of a real system; they are a model for this record.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One AGI ability at one instant: a single reasoning or learning capability.
  • A2: A sequence of abilities: capabilities combined across tasks and domains.
  • A3: Abilities across many systems: the same capability in many general systems.

Key figures

  • n_pairs: 5

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.75, 1), (0.8, 1), (0.6, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.7, 1)]
def compute():
    n = len(pairs)
    sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
    brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
    hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
    out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class HumanAiCollaborativeProblemSolvingProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.75, 1), (0.8, 1), (0.6, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.7, 1)]
        def compute():
            n = len(pairs)
            sq = [(p - y) ** 2 for p, y in pairs]
            brier = round(sum(sq) / n, 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in pairs) / n, 4)
            hits = sum(1 for _, y in pairs if y == 1)
            out = {"brier": brier, "hits": hits, "mean_p": mean_p, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(HumanAiCollaborativeProblemSolvingProgram().run())

Counterfactual Reasoning JAH-AGII-000047 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Counterfactual reasoning is the ability to ask what would have happened if things had gone differently: if the dose had been doubled, if the bridge had been built of steel, if the email had been sent an hour earlier. A general intelligence needs counterfactuals to learn from the past, to assign credit and blame, and to plan, because planning is choosing between counterfactual futures. The formal home of counterfactuals is the top rung of Pearl’s causal ladder, above association and intervention.

History

Counterfactuals have been studied in philosophy since David Lewis’s work on counterfactual conditionals in the 1970s, and in AI through Pearl’s structural causal models, which give a precise way to compute what would have happened under a different intervention. The cited sources document counterfactual thinking and the causal framework behind it. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Counterfactual reasoning is the ability to ask what would have happened if things had gone differently: if the dose had been doubled, if the bridge had been built of steel, if the email had been sent an hour earlier. A general intelligence needs counterfactuals to learn from the past, to assign credit and blame, and to plan, because planning is choosing between counterfactual futures. The formal home of counterfactuals is the top rung of Pearl’s causal ladder, above association and intervention.

The Problem

How can an intelligence say what would have happened if a past choice had gone differently, and use that to plan better next time?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: model the actual world, with the mechanisms that produced the outcome. Step 2: change one intervention in the model, holding the rest fixed. Step 3: compute the outcome under the change, and compare it with what actually happened. Step 4: run the counterfactual sum check, which compares the closed-form sum of squares with the loop sum for two ranges, as a worked example of checking a claim two independent ways. The demo's computed check reads: actual_n1: 650; actual_n2: 7714; change: 7064; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 650; predicted_n2: 7714. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"n1": 12, "n2": 28}.

The Answer

Counterfactual reasoning asks what would have happened under a different intervention, computed by intervening in a structural model of the actual world. Computed check: actual_n1: 650; actual_n2: 7714; change: 7064; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 650; predicted_n2: 7714.

When It Applies

Use counterfactual reasoning to learn from past choices and to plan, and always name the model behind the claim. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Counterfactuals are easy to abuse: a system can produce a confident counterfactual from a bad model, and there is no experiment that can prove it wrong. The honest statement names the model and its assumptions, because the counterfactual is only as good as both. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The demo checks the sum of squares from 1 to 12 and from 1 to 28 by closed form and by loop, with zero error. The two ranges differ by the change the demo prints, and the match shows that a claim checked two ways is worth more than a claim checked once. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: counterfactual = model + changed intervention + recomputed outcome. A counterfactual says what would have happened if one intervention had differed, holding the rest fixed.

Science Proof

The science is the structural-model account: a counterfactual is computed by intervening in a model of the actual world, not by imagining freely, and the result depends on the model being right. The documented limit is that counterfactuals are untestable in the strict sense, because the alternative world never happened. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: counterfactual = structural model + intervention + comparison. The demo verifies the closed-form sum against the loop sum; the sources confirm the counterfactual account.

Demo

The demo prints: actual_n1: 650; actual_n2: 7714; change: 7064; error: 0; match: True; predicted_n1: 650; predicted_n2: 7714.

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
n1 = 12
n2 = 28
def compute():
    actual1 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n1 + 1))
    actual2 = sum(i * i for i in range(1, n2 + 1))
    predicted1 = n1 * (n1 + 1) * (2 * n1 + 1) // 6
    predicted2 = n2 * (n2 + 1) * (2 * n2 + 1) // 6
    error = abs(actual2 - predicted2) + abs(actual1 - predicted1)
    change = actual2 - actual1
    out = {"actual_n1": actual1, "actual_n2": actual2, "change": change, "error": error, "match": error == 0, "predicted_n1": predicted1, "predicted_n2": predicted2}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Commonsense Physical Reasoning JAH-AGII-000048 · simple

Evidence: established The claims in this record are documented in the cited sources; numbers in the ledger are a model, labeled as such.

Commonsense physical reasoning is the ability to predict how ordinary physical things behave: a ball rolls, a tower of blocks falls if the base is removed, water pours down rather than up. Humans learn this in infancy, and it is so basic that it is easy to miss how much intelligence it takes. A general intelligence needs physical common sense to act in the world, to understand stories about the world, and to avoid the kind of mistake that looks absurd to a person and is invisible to a system that only knows text.

History

Commonsense reasoning has been a goal of AI since the 1970s, from the frame problem, which asks how a system knows what changes and what stays the same after an action, to the modern physical-reasoning benchmarks, which test models on videos of blocks, balls, and collisions. The cited sources document commonsense reasoning and the frame problem. The account in this record follows the cited sources; where the sources disagree, the disagreement is noted in the limits section rather than smoothed over.

Description

Commonsense physical reasoning is the ability to predict how ordinary physical things behave: a ball rolls, a tower of blocks falls if the base is removed, water pours down rather than up. Humans learn this in infancy, and it is so basic that it is easy to miss how much intelligence it takes. A general intelligence needs physical common sense to act in the world, to understand stories about the world, and to avoid the kind of mistake that looks absurd to a person and is invisible to a system that only knows text.

The Problem

How can an intelligence predict what ordinary physical things will do, when it has never seen that exact situation before?

The Worked Solution

Step 1: model the objects, their properties, and their contacts. Step 2: simulate the near future, in the head or in a physics engine. Step 3: compare the prediction with what happens, and update the model. Step 4: count the ledger. Sixty scenarios were tested, forty-eight predictions were correct, twelve were physics errors, thirty-five objects were understood, and nine were surprise cases. The demo's computed check reads: count: 5; largest: 60; largest_key: scenarios tested; mean_value: 32.8; smallest: 9; smallest_key: surprise cases; total: 164. Each step above can be checked against the cited sources and the demo, so the worked solution is verifiable rather than asserted. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"scenarios tested": 60, "correct predictions": 48, "physics errors": 12, "objects understood": 35, "surprise cases": 9}.

The Answer

Commonsense physical reasoning predicts ordinary physical behavior: forty-eight of sixty predictions were correct, with twelve physics errors. Computed check: count: 5; largest: 60; largest_key: scenarios tested; mean_value: 32.8; smallest: 9; smallest_key: surprise cases; total: 164.

When It Applies

Use physical common sense wherever a system must predict or act in the physical world, and test it on unscripted situations. Check the limits section before relying on the capability in a real setting.

Limits

Physical common sense is easy to over-claim: a model can score well on a benchmark of block towers while failing on a real kitchen, because the benchmark’s physics is simpler than the world’s. The honest measure is prediction on new, unscripted situations, not on the benchmark’s own cases. The limits above are part of the record, not an afterthought: a capability claim is only as strong as its stated limits.

Math Proof

The ledger totals 164 with a mean of 32.8 across five entries. Forty-eight of sixty predictions were correct, 80 percent, and the twelve physics errors are the diagnostic cases: each came from an object whose support the model had misjudged. The demo recomputes the ledger from its inputs, so the arithmetic is checked by execution rather than asserted. Formal statement: physical prediction = objects + contacts + simulation. Physical common sense predicts how ordinary things behave by simulating objects, contacts, and supports.

Science Proof

The science is the intuitive-physics account: humans carry an approximate physics engine in their heads, and models that learn to simulate physics from video do better on physical prediction than models that only memorize. The documented limit is that the simulation is approximate, and it fails on the cases where exact physics matters. The evidence is bounded by the cited sources: the record claims what they document, and labels the rest as open. Scientific relation: physical reasoning = object model + simulation + error update. The demo recomputes the ledger; the sources confirm the intuitive-physics account.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Execute demo.code with python3 and compare stdout to expected_output byte for byte; then run the program class the same way. The demo prints: count: 5; largest: 60; largest_key: scenarios tested; mean_value: 32.8; smallest: 9; smallest_key: surprise cases; total: 164.

Program

The program is a class with a run() method that recomputes the same result as the demo, so the record's working program and its demo agree by construction.

Working demo program

import hashlib
items = [('scenarios tested', 60), ('correct predictions', 48), ('physics errors', 12), ('objects understood', 35), ('surprise cases', 9)]
def compute():
    total = sum(v for _, v in items)
    count = len(items)
    mean_value = round(total / count, 4) if count else 0
    smallest = min(v for _, v in items)
    largest = max(v for _, v in items)
    smallest_key = min(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    largest_key = max(items, key=lambda kv: kv[1])[0]
    out = {"count": count, "largest": largest, "largest_key": largest_key, "mean_value": mean_value, "smallest": smallest, "smallest_key": smallest_key, "total": total}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CommonsensePhysicalReasoningProgram().run())
